POULTRY FOR PROFIT.

THE POULTRY YARD IN MAY.

Until a few years ago May was quite one of the busiest months of the whole year in the utilitarian's poultry yard, as most of the stock birds _required \ for next season's use were hatched during the. latter part of April or in May. Thanks to the enormous strides that arti- ficial incubation -and rearing have made, only comparatively few eggs are now set so late as this, nearly all the hatching for this season having been completed by the middle of last month.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
The Adult Stock.

A hen's most profitable period is during her first and second seasons. Generally speaking, unless the bird is particularly valuable, she-does not pay to keep after she is two or two7and-a- quarter 'years old. As soon as she approaches her second moult—for the change from chicken 1 to adult feathering in the first season is not a ' moult proper—she should be disposed of, other- wise every week after this time is lessening the amount of profit. Occasionally individual hens have laid remarkably well in their third, and even in the their fourth, year. As a matter of fact, they are productive up to six or seven years old. Regarded from a broad standpoint, it will be found that there is very little differ- ence in the number of eggs produced in their first and second years, but in the third year there is quite a considerable falling off. It Inay be said that the meridian is reached when the hens are two years and three months old. By the time this age is attained the hens should be nearing their second moult; this is, of course, provided that they were hatched at the right time of year, since this is of vital importance. from the commercial aspect. The Growing Chickens.

Careful management is needed in the case of half-grown chickens, for once development is checked one may be pretty sure that there is something wrong somewhere. It is important for the poultry-keeper to consider for what pur- pose his chickens are intended. If for stock pur- poses, thew on no account should development be forced, for birds whose growth has been. forced seldom make satisfactory stock birds. If they are to be marketed, on the ether hand, immediately they are large enough they may be forced to almost any extent, so long as health and vigour are maintained. Stock birds whose development has been unduly encouraged during the first few months of their-lives are very un- likely to throw strong and vigorous chickens, while they are at all times liable to produce infertile eggs.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Hints for Hot Weather.

The provision of abundant shelter for poultry of all kinds and of all ages is extremely im- portint. Too much sun is as bad as too little, and it is necessary to provicte a place in which the birds can find relief from the scorching heat. When possible, the coops or houses should be placed near a wood, for there is no cooler place than this during hot weather. It is not every poultry-keeper, however, who is fortunate enough to have such a place available, and in this case artificial shade should be ar- ranged. This is quite easy of accomplishment. Flake hurdles placed about the fields afford ample protection. Two ordinary hurdles tied together, with straw between, likewise answer the purpose. It is very important during hot weather to renew the drinking water frequently, for it soon becomes tepid and stale. The drink- ing vessels should be placed in the shade—under a hurdle referred to above is a very suitable spot—and in such circumstances the renewal of water twice a day is ample. Some foods are much more suitable during hot weather than -cold, and vice versa. The poultry-keeper wha varies the diet according to the time of year is much more likely to achieve good results. A ration which is eminently suitable during hot weather may be quite unfit during the depth of winter, while some foods that give satisfactory results during cold weather are positively dan- gerous when it is very hot. The best grains for hot weather are wheat, oats, and buckwheat, and the best meals, ground oats, middlings and bran. Green food is a very important item in the diet of poultry during the summer months. "Farm and Home."

THIS WEEK'S WORK IN BRIEF. Give the, chickens all theiiberty possible,And let a large pihportion of thjo mash be of green- food. The quantity of meat or animal food should be reduced. Natural food will be plentiful in the meadows, and the growing stock must be en- * couraged to forage for it. The mash should be light and bulky, and the last feed should always be of grain. Wheat itself is a fine food, as is two-thirds wheat and one-third oats. The box for packing eggs for setting should he about three and a half inches deep, and pre- ferably divided into compartments. Bran and sawdust are both suitable material for packing. See that there are no crevices in the box. Wrap each egg in soft paper, and keep as far apart as possible. Let them be equi-distant from the top and bottom of box, and see that they do not touch the sides. Let the lid of the box fit as closely as pos-' sible. Wrap the box in straw or hay, and enclose in stout brown paper. Advise the purchaser of dispatch.—" Poultry World."

LITTLE THINGS THAT COUNT. Chickens and Cleanliness.

If chickens are to be strong and healthy they must be kept perfectly clean, for no matter how well they are fed, if their roosting-places are dirty they will not thrive. It is therefore essential for their welfare that the droppings be removed each day, and the litter renewed as often as necessary, and the coops should now and again have a coat of limewash.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
Insects.

Poultry-keepers should ever be on the look-out for insects, and if such be found on the chickens steps should be taken at once to clear them away. Chickens cannot make progress if they are infested with these pests. The bodies of the chickens should be dusted with sulphur or insect powder. One of the worst parasites that attack the wee ones is the tick, and this insect generally makes its appearance when the chickens are about a fortnight old. These ticks appear on the lead and neck, and by fixing -xiime.-es on to the skin simply draw out the . 1:14.',45. and often enough cause the chickens t. go into a decline. Chickens infested with these pests should have the parts affected lightly rubbed with camphorated oil or some insect- destroying ointment. Fowls with Diarrhea.

• When fowls show a tendency to be afflicted with this complaint they should have all their -soft food mixed with cold water. If this plan is regularly carried out the bowels will soon assume a normal condition. It is a simple yet effective cure for this complaint.—" Feathered World."

FARM AND GARDEN.

MILKLESS COWS.

Agalactia or the non-production or absence of milk in the mammary gland, is met with in all animals, but more particularly in the cow and mare. This absence of secretion, says a writer in "Farm and Home," may occur even when the animal appears to be perfectly healthy, when parturition has been normal and gestation has reached its full limit, but in the majority of cases there is to be found some cause for it other than a want of development in the lacteal ap- paratus. In the case of a heifer or cow, it is generally policy to hand-rear the calf or put it to another cow, and to tie up the mother or send her on grass to fatten, or, if that is not con- venient, to sell her as a barren for someone else to fatten. A cow that has no milk at calving is not likely to become a record milker, and it does not pay to keep those whose yield is below the average. The same course should be taken with any other animal capable of bang fattened for the butcher.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
THE POTATO TRADE.

Potatoes have been one of the cheapest articles of food since the war started, but they have recently taken a decisive upward turn in price. It would not be easy to say how far the rise is attributable to actual scarcity or to Rifficulths of transport, but it may fairly be assumed that uncertain railway service is the prlcipal factor. The country is normally self-sufficient in pota- toes, the bulk of the imports consisting of the early supplies that are scarcely distinguishable from luxuries, while at least corresponding quantities remain on the farms to be consumed by stock or go to waste. There is, however, evidence of shortage, present or anticipated, in the fact that the 'farmer is able to command a liberal proportion of the higher price paid by the consumer. 'The grower has waited long for this turn in the market, and in view of the in- creased cost of production it cannot be said that his reward is unduly liberal. Indeed, it may be a fortunate thing for the country .that the market has changed while planting is -still in progress. Had things remained; as, they were, it is not improbable that the area would have

been curtailed in favour of sotne other crop, the value of which had risen in closer harmony with the outlay entailed in its production. The recent rise of El or more per ton will give a timely stimulus, and enable those who were hesitating to come to a favourable decision.— "Field."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW COLDS ARE CONTRACTED.

The common idea is that colds and the bron- chial and pulmonary troubles invariably result from exposure to cold, wet, and inclement weather. In some cases they may arise from these causes, but the majority of colds are con- tracted from breathing a close, impurd atmo- sphere. It is common experience among horse- keepers that a horse used to a warm stable and clothing, and perhaps newly-shorn of its coat, seems to take no harm when turned out in cold weather, provided that it is well fed, while the same animal, brought from apparently uncom- fortable conditions outside, exposed to wet and every wind that blows, and placed in a stable without horses, almost certainly develops a bad cold and cough. In bringing colts in for break- ing purposes, it is a bad plan to put them in a stable with other horses. They are pretty sure to go wrong, and this, of course, interferes with their training. They should be put into a shed or roomy box that is well ventilated, and thus gradually accustomed to the change from a life of freedom in the open air.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

CHRYSANTHEMUMS.

Now is the time for planting the above. They should be got in as quickly as possible. Old plants may be split up and strong rooted shoots planted out. It is always best to grow young plants, because larger blooms can be got, and they flower earlier. No special soil is neces- sary, but one that was well manured last season with leaf mould added is • the ideal soil for chrysanthemums. Beginners cannot do better than grow The Masse family. This class gives most blooms and good one and is very easy to grow; height about 21ft.; flowering in August and September. Stake up as planted, but it is best to put stakes in first, then the roots will not get torn; 4ft. canes are the best, obtainable from the local seedsmen.

The following varieties are about the best dozen : .

Framfield Early White.—Massive flowers, pure white; disbud; flower September, October; height 4ft. - - .

foi des Blanes.—Fine pure white; August, September; 2-ift. Abercorn Beauty.—Orange bronze, sport from Polly, real beauty; disbud; August, September; 2ft.

_ Goachers Crimson.—The finest crimson in this section, yellow reverse; disbud; August, September; 2ft. Bronze Goacher.—Sport from above, identical except colour. Improved Masse.—Large mauve flowers, good shape; does well in sprays or disbudded; August, September; 24ft. Horace Martin.—Another Masse; wants beat- ing as a yellow; rich buttercup yellow; good all round variety; August September; 21ft. Hector.—Lovely mauve-pink, long florets on long stems; beautiful; September; 3ft. Almirante.—Red, crimson and bronze, large flowers; disbud, grand variety; September, October; 3ft. Provence.—Rose, golden centre, large flowers, good grower; August, September; 2ift. Perks Chatillonaise.—Lovely creamy white, shaded rose flowers; good any way; October, 4ft. Golden Glow.—Very large incurved, canary yellow, vigorous grower; must be disbudded; best of the incurved varieties; September, October; 4ft. Good dwarf varieties are : Goachers Crim- son, crimson; Bronze Goacher, bronze; Polly, orange; Abercorn Beauty, orange-bronze; Plue de Argent, white, tinted pink; Diana, •bronze, gold tipped; Leslie, yellow; Crimson Polly, crimson.

The above are very good for forming back- ground in beds, and all are very floriferous and jarge flowers. CEVO.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNNER BEANS.

It is not advisable to sow or plant cut runner beans before the early part of May. To hasten the crop seeds may be sown in boxes and placed under glass in genial warmth, and be finally planted out when well hardened off in deeply trenched rich soil as soon as all danger of frost is past. Many people plant in trenches, and this is a very good way, as then it is possible occasionally to flood the rows with water should the weather be at all dry. If runner beans ara allowed to get dry at the roots the flowers always fail to set. Those who garden near towns often find it very difficult to procure tall stakes as used for peas, in which case poles may be used with advantage. If carefully stored away dur- ing the winter in a dry shed they will last for many years. These poles should, if they can be had, be not less than eight feet long, so that when fixed in the ground, each pole may be some seven feet high. They should be lashed to a horizontal rod stretched from the one pole to the other. The poles may be fixed at a distance of two feet from each other, this allowing pldnty of room for the plants, which should on no account be crowded. When grown in this way runner beans form an admirable screen for hiding any unsightly object. If stakes cannot be procured then the runner bean may be grown in dwarf form, as is done in market gardens. When grown thus the plants must be frequently stopped, and a mulch of strawy manure placed between the lines so as to prevent the pods being spoiled by coming in contact with the soil.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
TUFTED PANSIES NEWLY PLANTED.

Many people hesitate to pick off the flowers from their pansies when they plant them out. This is a mistake, as it hinders the roots from taking to their fresh quarters, and the flowers and buds if left also weaken the plants and check the production of bloom later on in the season. The great point is to get the plants established in their new position as speedily as may be, and every means should be taken to bring this about. When the flowers are removed there is, of course, a laok of bloom for a short time, but the wisdom of the practice will be apparent in the larger plants and the freer dis- play of bloom that will be forthcoming. All the buds and open blossoms should be removed.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 13, 191 G. FARMERS AND MILITARY. JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Lady Church Officials. I presume that last week's suggestion that there should be women labour in this as in other departments of life is intended to apply to the immediate locality, for there are already a few lady " sidesmen " elsewhere. I worked several years in an area which embraced the interesting old hill village of Heptonstall, which is said to have suffered assault in the Cromwellian period, and a former colleague who is still in that, district informs me that at the Easter vestry meeting ladies were elected to the position referred to, and have since assisted in the collection of the offertory.

Not Scottish Ye Ken. Private Ernest Shaw's explanation as to why he joined the Cameron Highlanders set me thinking of certain other young fellows person- ally known to myself who, born and bred in Lancashire, chose to enlist in the King's Own Scottish Borderers. One of these injured him- self in leaping over a fence, and was conse- quently detained in hospital while his com- rades proceeded to France. There he was dis- covered by an old bandmaster anxious to fill a vacancy, and, being a versatile and skilful musician, he was transferred to the band and was for some time engaged in recruiting work. But when he felt well and fit, he soon began to chafe under these restraints. As he put it to me when on furlough, "How can I with a good conscience continue to play off for the Front lads who have joined since I did?" So he rested not until, the order came to proceed on active service.

One More Unfortunate. In like manner there came a period when not a few others with whom I am acquainted sud- denly took a fancy to the Devons. although they were natives of Lancashire, and had not been much out of this County Palatine. On account of one of these my soul is grievously distressed. He was on the staff of a newspaper on which I worked a while, and I sincerely admired him with good reason. Tie was among the first enthusiasts to join the Colours, and was engaged, as a bomber in the grand assault on HiIT 60— since which historic event his parents have been unable to obtain any sort of tidings respecting his fate. "Truly Polite." Last week's little story of the extremely polite man somehow reminded me of an experience of my own in the early 'seventies. I had been sent to a little town in the Yorkshire heavy woollen district to report a lecture on Disestab- lishment by a then .very popular agent of the Liberation Society. It was to be given in what was called Bethesda Methodist New Connexion Schoolroom, though, of course, there is now no such denomination. The place was strange to me, and I was obliged to seek direction. Accosting a couple—mother and daughter to all appearance—l asked as to the way I ought to take. _ _ _

"Bethesda skooil?" repeated the elder to the younger inquiringly; " duz he theean. t' Ranters, thinks tag" _ . _

For reply, the latter stopped a passer-by— another lady—and submitted the problem to her; and the newcomer promptly solved it after this manner :

_ " Bethesda ? Ah ; yes, yes, to be sure. You proceed in this direction perhaps a hundred yards or so, and then, turning to the right, you will doubtless find a number of people wending their way thither!" Good old "wending." How useful many a young reporter has found it in the course of the 40 and odd years that have since elapsed.

-Is Silence Always Golden? It was in that same part of the country that story was current of two brothers who worked long side by side and never spoke to each other. An old friend of mine, Air. Levi Haigh, repro- duces the story thus :—They were dr; wallers. One day they were engaged building a wall near a hayfield, and, after finishing their mid-day meal, they spent the rest of the dinner-hour 1 among the bay. - Each took held of a rake ani began, in fun, to hit the other, but after a time .changed to intended blows. Leaving the hay- field they returned to their work, never to speak to each other again. Day'after day, month after month, and for thirty years they never ex- !changed a word. Sometimes they faced each other as they built the walls, and, if ever one required the help of his brother he would stand by till he saw him and came to render assistance. This strange "state of things continued up to the death of one of the brothers, when the other attended his funeral. Another Example. I removed thence over the border into Lanca- shire, and my very nest situation revealed to me a similar instance. A wealthy brother and sister lived together in that district, and, as the house wastlonely and they saw little company, no wonder the lady now and again betrayed affection for the curate for the time being. Finally her brother remonstrated with her in terms so vigorous and unmistakable that the offended lady there and then vowed she would never speak to him again. They continued to live togtther, and she served well and faithfully as housekeeper just as if there had been no breach; but never a word spake either to the other until death did them part. ,

Unflattering Version. The " front " name of the Labour organiser cited in last week's " Gossip " reminded rile Of another Adam whom I knew not far short of half a century ago. He was a tailor, Adam Lowe by name. I was once passing his place when his somewhat aggressive signboard had arrested the attention of two of the illiterate class, then so common. One was laboriously spelling it out to the other, and I heard him say: " ' A dam low tailor.' Well, of all! Nay, nay, I never did!" Funeral Customs.

The story in the same column as to "No flowers by request" (from which text, as some may remember, I held forth a while ago) set my thoughts upon the general subject. I can go back to a period when hearses were surmounted by immense plumes, and the coachmen had tre- mendous mourning bands to their hats, and the coffins were mostly black. And, although these oppressive trappings of woe—l believe that is the proper phrase in this connection—have mean- while gradually given place to less sombre evidences of grief, funeral customs remain essentially the same in all country places of which I have experimental knowledge. I sup- pose that, as a matter of fact, advancing civilisation nevertheless, these have continued practically unchanged through the centuries. Looking Backward. I wonder if many of my younger readers happen to be blessed with or to have acquired a taste for the Waverley novels. Some who have may remember how, describing the funeral banquet of the fallen Saxon chief Athelstane, Sir Walter Scott observes:—

axons on these "Indeed, the ideas of the S occasions were as natural as they were rude. If sorrow was thirsty, there was drink; if hungry, there was food; if it sunk down upon and sad- dened the heart, here were the means supplied of mirth, or at least, of amusement. Nor did assistants scorn to avail themselves of these means of consolation." _ .

One does not care to be misunderstood, and I should be loth to hurt the feelings of any lately bereaved, but really—well, after all, it is-prob- able that it has always been substantially so in all countries and in all ages; but reformers long for a time of extreme simplification of th,! customs attending the last sad rites. As a hint to whomsover it may concern, let me repeat that my own ultimate destination is the crematorium; and I cannot understand the repugnance to it manifestly so common.

"And Now, Brethren." Notwithstanding what I have just written, I ".would not omit some of the vanities and varieties of other people and nations . differing from each other in manners as well as in mat- ters of estate, government, religion and policies." I quote from a work entitled "Of the Antiquity of Ceremony Used at Funerals," by Sir , William Dethick, who conducted that of Mary com- mend "Theeenof e ScoAths'h(octo,,n,cteirinouinggh "The Monastery " whom let me of which it is the sequel, should, of course, be read first). Our author finds that " antiently most people have consumed their dead bodies sintrofinrg f'e.'eir After ter satosmtaateehmeonft twh average reader, might be too he adds: "Some people used immoderate laugh. ter at the funeral of theyre friends, and on the contrary, the Irish nation exceed all others in their howlings and lamentations." And so one might go on, but for the present, 'nuff said.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
Practical Men Urgently Required For Land.

SONS WHO DO NOT "GAD ABOUT."

The Warrington and District War Agricul- tural Committee are anxious to fulfil their obligations to increase the food supply of the country, but the members foresee grave difficul- ties unless the present shortage of skilled farm labour is at once grappled with, and the military authorities see their way to allow every eligible farm hand to remain in his present occupation.

The position of the farmers was discussed at a specially convened meeting of the committee, held at the Griffin Hotel, Warrington, on Wed- nesday evening, and one member contended that it was the duty of every farmer to make the strongest representations possible, and to use every available means to point out to the mili- tary authorities• that although the war had to be carried on, and victorS, had to be won at all costs, it was quite essential that the land should be cultivated, not only as well as it had been in the past, but even better if possible, and that they should have all the available men they could get. They wanted men of experience— those who understood farming—to produce the best crops possible. It was all very well for people to say there was a farmer with two, three, or four sons, but his contention was that there were not men available to take up farm work in this district on Saturday afternoons. Sundays, and evenings. In these days men would have Saturday afternoons and Sundays to them- selves. The only available men to look after the farming interests were the farmers' sons. Farming was their occupation and their living, and they knew everything that was required for the proper cultivation of the land. If all the young men were taken from the land they could not possibly produce the food that was vital necessity'.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGATIONS RE POTATO PRICES REPUDIATED.

Then the speaker went on to refer to what he described as a great shout about the price of potatoes at the present time. "I have heard it said the farmers are holding potatoes back to get a bigger price. That is false, from be- ginning to end," he declared with emphasis. Anyone who knew anything about farming was aware that the potato stocks were smaller than they had been for years past. The reason for the big increase in' the price of potatoes was due to the fact that the demand exceeded the. supply. He then 'volunteered the very startling state- ment that that day there was not a single farmer in the Warrington Market with a single waggon of potatoes. It was an entirely wrong idea for the people to get it into their heads that farmers were responsible for the enhanced prices. If there was a scarcity of this produee after the first year of war, and the young 'skilled farm haas were taken away, how could they be expected to increase the production? He ventured to say that the outlook would be far 'worse next year. Owing to the shortage of labour farmers had been compelled to put less land under cultivation than was the case last year. Another side of the question of labour shortage was put forward, by a member, who said farmers had to make their plans for the cultivation f the land in good time, and 'in making these arrangements this year they had to be guided by the shortage of labour. "It will be impossible to get in the crops that are grown. unless we get more men than we have at the present time," declared another farmer. He urged that something ought to be done to obtain Irish or Danish labour. although he admitted that it was well-nigh impossible to get the former.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
FARMERS' INDUSTRIOUS SONS.

It was contended by another farmer that the skilled man was taken and the man who was practically useless was left. One speaker repudiated the suggestion that farmers' sons in this area went "gadding about like gentlemen." The sons of a Lancashire farmer had- to buckle to as soon as they left school, and sometimes iefore. There were a lot of people who could be patriotic with other people's sons. Unless something were done to restrict the taking of their practical men, they could not hope to increase the food supply. "Leave us with our practical men, whether they are our sons or not, and we can manage with women and boys," was the speaker's con- cluding suggestion to the military authorities. COMPETITION WITH WEALTHY FIRMS FOR LABOUR.

Another farmer said it had been thrown at th-1 farming community that they were not patriotic, while the big works were allowing the men to join the Colours. Consequently, there had been a big demand for men over military age. Good practical men had been tempted to go into the works on being offered big money. Farmers had been crippled because the Govern- ment had .stipulatied what price they should re- ceive for their produce, and they were not able to keep pace with the increasing cost of produc- tion and increased wages. I>eople spoke of war prices, but farmers had sold produce at pretty nearly the same price, occasionally, as in normal times. Farmers did not want unreason- able prices, but he considered they should receive what was considered fair and equitable. The continued drain on agricultural labour would. be very serious for the country in the future. From a business point of view farmers ought to have a higher price for their produce in order to go on producing. The discussion then ended.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
LOOKING AFTER FARMS.

Committee Condemns Neglect Of

Cultivation.

At a previous meeting of the committee, held at the Griffin Hotel, Mr. Joshua Ball presiaed. A representative on this committee has been elected for each township in the Warrington Rural District Area and the surrounding dis- tricts using Warrington as market town, and farmers are requested to report to their repre- sentative any special difficulties they have to contend with in the cultivation of their hold- ings in this period of national stress. Mr. John Bridge (Glazebrook) was unani- mously elected to represent the committee on the Warrington Rural District Tribunal, and Mr. Jos. Shaw (Newton-le-Willows) on the War- rington Borough Tribunal. The Secretary reported that he had received satisfactory replies to his communications to various landowners re destruction of dangerous weeds, rdoks, &c. Various cases of absolute neglect of cultiva- tion (specially of small holdings and isolated fields) were reported, and the Secretary was re- quested to write to the owners and tenants on these matters. Drastic action on the part of the Board of Agriculture would be advised if im- provements were not speedily made. Certain areas in the locality now lying almost unpro- ductive owing to flooding through ill-kept brooks were pointed out; and the county authorities were requested to investigate these cases with a view to taking immediate action in compelling improvements to drainage. In order to assist the Government in the compilation ( f reliable statistics as to the food production of the country, the committee wish, to impress upon ever), farmer the necessity of fully and accurately filling up the June forms of the Board of Agriculture re cropping, Ste., and a 'resolution to the county committee was sent asking that they recommend the Board to make compulsory the due filling up of these forms. A circular letter from the Board of Agricul- ture re inspection of farms not producing as much as they ought to do if farmed carefigly and systematically was considered, and it was resolved to leave the further consideration of this matter till next meeting.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

Men Who Are Looking Forward To Walking Day.

READY FOR THE "BIG PUSH."

Since the war began our lads have buoyed themselves up with anticipations that they would be home for the next festival. Festivals, however, have come and gone, and they are still away. They now hope for release by Walk- ing Day. First in an excellent collection of letters pub- lished in this month's Orford Parish Magazine is one stating: "All the lads are in the best of health, hoping you are the same. Cheero! We shall all be home for Walking Day with a bit of luck." And then follow stirring epistles from France and the East. Says one: "We have been here a few weeks now, and it is not so bad a place, considering it is so near the firing line. There are a few houses that have been 'bumped' but one can't expect any- thing else from the Bosches; especially if there is a church anywhere near, as a church always seems their objective when they are shelling. I am glad to say that we have had two very nice Church services while we have been here, and the Rev. Mr. 'Collier carried on the ser- mons. He is a very nice chaplain."

GRATEFUL FOR PRAYERS.

And another:

"To think that prayers are offered up on so many occasions at your church for us gives me great encouragement to carry out my duties without any fear of death. . . . It was only a few days ago that I got my first taste of being under shell fire. To tell you the truth, I can hardly believe that the men out here realise the fact that danger is ahead of them daily, because they seem to work smoothly, no matter what conditions they are working under. Taking active service work all through, it is pretty rough, but I keep my heart in the right place and keep on smiling. The food is rough, the sleeping accommodation is rough, but I know it is all in the game, and I shall get back to my accustomed life some day, with God's help. When I do come home I promise you to make your Bible Class increase. . . . lam sure all the boys out here are looking forward confi- dently to the time when we start to make the big push, and I may state the Huns will know about it. We have plenty of guns and shells and men, which will beat the Huns any time they like. • So good-bye and God bless you all."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
TAKING THINGS AS THEY COME.

And these letters also: "In the trenches for the first time it was a bit rough, but I am getting quite used to it now. Our company has had very few casualties, thank God, up to the present time. It is mar- vellous how some have escaped certain death. The best thing a man can do out here is to put his trust in God and take things as they come." "We were on our travels passing through a part of France where English troops had not been since the time of Napoleon. We have been in the trenches now for fifteen days. If we had remained at the rest camp two days longer we should have been confirmed. Good- ness knows when we will again have the chance."

"Things are very busy here now, and victory is drawing closer I am sure. The French are confident, and their splendid defence of Ver- dun proves their efficiency. With regard to us, nothing could be better. The section I am in deals with machine guns and parts and the French helmets, which some misguided people at home are worrying about." "We have at last arrived somewhere in France. Luckily we steered clear of the beastly sub's, landed and came on here. We were 60 hours on the journey. The men are billeted in a barn, and are fairly comfortable. I have a room in a farmhouse which is quite nice. The people are Belgian refugees. I am afraid I shall have to rub up my French a good deal more before I shall be able to make myself understood by the people here. It is very amusing to see our fellows trying to make them- selves understood. Don't know how long we shall be here, but expect it won't be many days before we are facing the music."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIERS' LIFE IN THE EAST.

"After being stuck in the trenches for nearly twelve months on the Peninsula, it is like living in a new world again. We have now taken up our new position, and the lads are slogging out for all they are worth, putting the place in a state of defence. We are up in the hills, 1,120 feet above sea-level. We have a splendid view of the surrounding country, very different from Ashi Baba staring you in the face from morn- ing until night. Thank God, He has put brighter scenes before our • eyes. The Turks themselves call the Peninsula the 'Butcher's Shop,' or 'Slaughter House' (this is from Turk- ish prisoners themselves), and I can honestly say these are very good names to call it by. Well, anyhow, the lads, don't want to see the place again for some considerable time, and I would not be surprised to hear a good many more say the same, those who have experienced the place. ,I was at Cape Helles side at first, with the Australians at Gaba Tepe, or Anzac. Well, I. think I had better put a Stop to all this concerning tile Peninsula. I suppose you have heard quite sufficient from the brave Orford lads who came forward without delay for King and country. I don't know how some of the young fellows .can hang hack so when there is more urgent business out here or nearer home in France. In a few years to come their sons and daughters will be asking them, 'What did you do in the Great War, daddy?' I wonder if their answer will be as slack coming as they themselves were. Give me the man who can answer that he came forward for King and country." "The weather here is champion; it gets hotter every day. I saw about twenty or thirty aero- planes flying over to Bulgaria the other week, but I don't know what damage they did. They looked excellent, too. I don't know when we are going to start fighting again. I hope we don't do any more in this country. I wish they would send us to some other part of the world now. There are plenty of mountains here which we could climb, but we can see no sport in climbing mountains. I think. we were camped in one of the prettiest spots in Macedonia before we came here. We were on the edge of a big lake, and there was a sulphur bath about two miles from camp."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN ON 1111 E LAND.

%Vliat Has Been Done And Is Being

Done In Cheshire.

A meeting of the Cheshire County Committee (in co-operation with the Board of Traue Labour Exchanges) to promote the employment of women in agriculture during the war, pre- sided over by Lord Newton, was held at the Crewe Arms Hotel on Saturday. The Hon. Secretary (the Hon. John E. Cross) reported that all the students trained at the Henhull hail Farm had obtained posts, and that arrangements had been made to increase the number of 'students trained. The Board of Agriculture has also decided to offer 20 free scholarships at this institution, and the ques- tion of a grant for farmers and others who are willing to provide training facilities is under consideration. It has been suggested that it might be pos- sible to arrange a demonstration of women's labour .at the Royal Show, and it was decided that the question should be considered in con- junction with the Lancashire Committee and a sub-committee nominated by the Show Com- mittee.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
Six Hundred Vacancies Filled.

The Hon. Secretary, reporting on the pro- gress of the village registration scheme, stated that 18 meetings had been held during the past month, and that nearly • 600 vacancies had been filled since the institution of the committee. He also stated that the appeal for further train- ing facilities had been very satisfactory, and that the present need was for candidates for the openings.. All women who register for agricultural work are entitled to a certificate on registration, signed by the Presidents of the Boards of 'Pratte and Agriculture and Fisheries, and the armlet is supplied* to all women who have registered and who have completed 30 days' work on the land on an aggregate since tbe war.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

The River Weaver.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM WESTON POINT TO NORTH WICH.

It will probably not have occurred to the 'Saturday afternoon cyclist starting from War- rington that a most interesting tour can be made along the course of the Weaver from the point at which it debouches into the Mersey, or to speak now more correctly, into the Manches- ter Ship Canal. Seldom does one see a cyclist at the village of Weston Point, yet not only is the place interesting as an old-world hamlet, but rich in historical associations. It is a van- tage ground from which may be viewed a grand panorama, in which the Frodsham and Relsby bluffs loom grandly in the middle ground. Mag- nificent, too, is here the broad expanse of the Mersey estuary, where, if the tide should hap- pen to be at full, and the evening sun gilding the high lights of the picture, the pausing cyclist will wonder why he has so long neg- lected to extend to this secluded spot a' run often cut short at Runcorn. Setting out, then, along to the road to Moore, the spin past the entrance to Norton Priory, with its plenitude of. trees—alas! fast disap- pearing under the upas-like influence of Widnes gases—and along the breezy open country to Runcorn, is by no means to be despised as lack- ing in picturesqueness, to say nothing of his- torical associations aroused by the grim ruins of Halton Castle on the left, and on the right by that grand old river, where, at the "Gap," ,was at one time a widely-known and highly- esteemed watering,place, now the commercial centre of the Mersey and the Bridgewater waterways. The cyclist will probably have to inquire at Runcorn the tortuous way to Weston which burrows under the station, and, bearing to the left, climbs to the high land, which ends abruptly at the "Point." It is suggested that the rider, turning now sharply backwards at Wes- ton Point, follows a track by Rock Savage and past Halton Station along the open road by Dutton Workhouse to Barnton and Winnington. This is one of the finest, as well as the quietest roads to be found within ten miles of Warring- ton. Furthermore, it has always something varied and picturesque to charm the artist eye in the ever-changing panorama of the Weaver valley, of which splendid views are seen at every turn of the road. Avoiding Northwich itself—of which one sees enough from the high ground at Anderton—a pleasant homeward route is found by way of Marbury Park, Cora- berlach and Stretton.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
CYCLE CAMPING.

Have you ever done a cycle camping tour? If not, there is a treat 'in store for you. It is the jolliest and cheapest way of spending a holiday one could wish for. There are none of the irksome restrictions that mar an ordinttry tour, when one must for ever be on the look- out for decent and reasonable sleeping quarters and feeding places. All roads are open; you may go as you please and when you please. The whole secret of cycle camping is to take just what you cannot pos- sibly do without and nothing else. Every ounce of weight you put on takes off some of your pleasures. My goods and chattles are carried in a waterproof hold-all strapped to the back carrier, and a basket resting on a special front carrier. This gives ample storage room for the complete outfit for a fortnight's tour. The front basket can be dispensed with for a short holiday. The tent—hired for a few shil- lings from one of the firms dealing in such things—is rolled tightly and strapped with the tent-pole to the top tube. A certain amount of ingenuity always has to be exercised in storing away one's kit, so that it is as well to practise several times before actually packing up for the tour. A sheet of American cloth round the chattels in the basket is very useful, otherwise wet has a way of trickling through and Spoiling everything.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
A CAMPING OUTFIT.

Here are some things which are liable to be forgotten, but which every camper of experi- ence will agree are absolutely indispensable : An extra pair of stockings and an extra vest; a box for soap; two towels; a spare pair of shoe laces; spirit stove (because you will sometimes not be allowed to make a fire on your camping site); bit of looking glass: corkscrew; scissors; tin-opener; housewife (containing needle, cot- ton, &c.); small mallet, for drivinc, in the tent- pegs; and waterproof ground sheet. All of these, of cout-se, are in addition to an enam- elled mug and plate; knives, forks and spoons; small frying-in; aluminium sauce-pan; billy- can; teapot; three small tins, containing lA- per, salt, and unmixed mustard; tins of jam or marmalade, tea, coffee or cocoa, and sugar; linen bag for bread (replenished daily at first likely spot); two blankets or down quilt; tent, tent-pegs, and guide ropes. It seems a lot, but it is wonderful what a small space these neces- sities will occupy once you have really set your wits to work.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
SIDECARS BY RAIL.

If you ever have occasion to send your sidecar outfit by rail it will pay, you to study carefully the regulations of, the railway companies. One thing it is most important to remember—always detach your sidecar. A striking illustration of the necessity for doing this was recently pub- lished. A motor cyclist, a member of the A.A. was quoted 30s. for sending his cycle and sidecar (unattached) from London to Scotland. The garage people did not carry out his instructions and forwarded the outfit attached. The member subsequently received a bill for £l3 Bs. 6d. Not wishing to hold the garage proprietor respon- sible, the member placed the matter in the hands of the Automobile Association. After communicating with the two companies con- cerned the A.A. was advised that the charge on this occasion would be reduced to £3 7s. 3d. This shows in a striking manner how necessary it is to be careful in such a matter.

WHERE FERRIES ARE WANTED.

In many parts of the country ferries aro badly needed. Some time ago when motoring

===== 0002601_19160513_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE TO HAVE THE PETROL TANK.

At the present time the petrol tank is placed in a variety of positions on different makes of cars. The earliest motor cars had their fuel tanks under the driving scat, the spirit being fed to the carburetter by gravity. This arrange- ment has certain very definite advantages. In this .position a tank can be made large enough to hold five, ten, or even twenty gallons, accord- ing to the size of the car. But what is even more important, it is, comparatively speaking, immune from damage either by fire or collision. Of course, to fill the tank necessitates disturbing a passenger, but enough spirit can generally be run- in at the c.ommencement, of a journey te 'last until the next. stop. During the last two or three years the' rear tank has found increasing, favour,, despite annoyance caused by clogged pressure pipes, ,filters and valves. But here the chief danger is the grave risk of collision, and a tank ornamented with several dents is be- coming quite a common sight. A protective cover of wooden lathes which is sometimes employed only lessens. but by no means obviates the possibility of damage. My own car has the petrol tank fixed on to the inside of the dash board. and this is about the best position there is I think.. It is easy to get at, it is by no means unsightly, and it is safe from collis- sions.

gld

in Lincolnshire I wanted to cross from Grimsby to Hull, and owing to the fact that there was no ferry I had to make a sixty mile detour. To make matters wore, the bridges in this part of the country are frequently too light for motor traffic. It is to he hoped that the Yorkshire and Lincolnshire authorities will join forces it an early date and provide an efficient ferry service between the two places, particularly as the pre- sent unsatisfactory state of affairs means that the north-eastern .portion of Lincolnshire is virtually shut off from the neighbouring dis- tricts. They manage, better in other parts of Great Britain. The Thames, for example, has a good ferry from Gravesend to Tilbury (although we would be thankful if it ran more frequently). The Firth of Forth can in normal times, be crossed from Granton to Burntisland, and the Clyde is easily negotiated via the ferry from Grenock to Helensburgh. The excellent ferry service from Liverpool to Birkenhead and Seacombe is known to all motorists in the north of England. Before the war there was a fre- quent ferry service between Portsmiuth and Gosport, avoiding a troublesome detour around the shores of Portsmouth Harbour, but for the moment it is suspended.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
TIME-EXPIRED MEN.

Sir,—As a regular reader of your valuable paper allow me to make a few remarks as re- gards time-expired men. • I had the luck to come home to Warrington after 17 months in the firing line, and I do not _Plink it is right either to call men up again or Keep them at the front when their time is up. , I am a patriotic man, but when one comes to took round and find hundreds of young fellows knocking round, who have not been to the Front, and don't intend if it is left to them, it lS time for us time-expired men to speak. I 13ay if they want to keep us, then keep us at home for a spell. The few gentlemen who are Pressing this Bill forward ought to go and have 17 and 20 months up in the line. I reckon there would be a different tale to tell. I say, give the -lads , about to become time-expired a chance, and what is more a rest.

_ . 'E. HOWARD, Ex-Sergeant 2nd South Lan.es. Regt. 70, Afersey-street, Warrington.

"PET OF THE ALMIGHTY."

Rev. J. Grange Bennett Condemns The

Kaiser For His Vanity.

In the course of a striking sermon, at the Silver-street Sunday School anniversary ser- vices on Sunday, the Rev. J. Grange Bennett condemned the Kaiser for his vain ambition to become a world-ruler, lie added that the Kaiser seemed to have imagined that he was a pet of the Almighty. In the whole of history there was not a more terrible example of what vanity and unrestrained ambition might lead to.

In the evening the reverend gentleman in- sisted upon the necessity of having faith in God. We were making a mistake if we just called for Dreadnoughts and ignored God, he declared.

Throughout the day special hymns were sung by the scholars, who were well arranged on a newly-erected platform. Mr. J. E. Hannan con- ducted and Mr. George Pendlebury presided at the organ. The choir successfully rendered "Send out Thy Light" (Gounod) and the .11orus, "Glorious is Thy Name," from Niozart's 12th Mass.

Dr. Bennett presided at the afternoon ser- vice and Mr. J. C. Holland delivered an inter- esting address. Messrs. J. Poole and J. Turton ably sang a duet from Stainer's "Crucifixion." The collections were a record—Xss 3s.

THE REV. T. VICKERS.

MISS BROADBENT AND MARRIED Departure From St. Clement's Solely NURSES. For Experience.

Sir,—Referring to Miss Broadbent's remarks At the meeting of the Board of Guardians in last Saturday's issue, she mentioned a married nurse (sister) as not being a good example to Young inexperienced girls. I will explain the facts of her appointment. Last year the sister referred to resigned her position at the White- 'cross Military Hospital to be married, and was to take up duties with the Q.A.I.M.N.S. on 'October Ist, 1915. After her marriage, at the Isequest of the Military Committee, she applied tu the War Office for release, and was granted release from the Q.A.I.M.N.S. on September to resume her previous position at the Whitecross Military Hospital. If the White- Tross Military Committee did not object, why should Miss Broadbent, considering she is not •011 the committee and has nothing to do with the said sister's appointment? think the public *will agree, whether single tn* married, it is the duty of every trained llurse, if possible, to help to restore our wounded leroes to health again. E.T.

The Rev. T. Vickers, who concludes his work as priest-in-charge of St. Clement's Mission on Tuesday, May 23rd, has an interesting reference to his departure in the Parish Church Magazine, where he states: "I am intensely grateful for all the love and kindness which have been shown me on all sides during my six and a half years' work here, and I am just as sincerely sorry to bring to an end the happiest days I have so' far spent. My object in going to work at the Parish Church, Liverpool, under Mr. Hockley, is solely to gain experience. Whatever has been done in the last six years has been done (in the words on the foundation stone of the new aisle) ' Gratia Dei,' by the Grace of God. I shall pray that St. Clement's will go on in the future to do many more things for, the Glory of God, by the Grace of God."

BRICK-STREET MISSION SCHOOL.

Mrs. Osborne And The Children's Welfare.

A very successful day commemorated the annual sermons in connection with the above Sunday school. Preceding the morning service, the band and scholars toured the neighbour- hood, and at 10.45 Mr. Rushton Silson, of Wigan, preached, the scholars and choir rendering special music. .

'At the afternoon service the united choirs of church and school rendered with pleasing taste the cantata "Nature's Voices." Mrs. E. Osborne presided, and the children presented flowers appropriate to their recitations to her. They formed, when, she had arranged them, an ex- ceedingly tasteful -centre.

Children and Flowers.

Mrs. Osborne, addressing the children, said Councillor Shaw assured her that she could please herself as to making a speech; but she could not refrain from saying how glad she was to be present at such a delightful service. "We have listened," she continued, "with the greatest possible pleasure to the sweet singing. What a happy idea it was to comingle children's voices with those of the seniors, conveying messages from the flowers of the field. Children and flowers to me are almost synonymous, inas- much as they represent purity. To flourish, both of them need the best possible care and attention. The soil must be properly prepared, they must be protected from unkind winds, they must have sunshine and rain, and their growth must not be hampered by evil weeds, that so, insidiously, are apt to intertwine them and pre- vent their , development. The garden is the natural home of the flower, and a good gardener watches over them with such care and interest that at the first sight of arrested growth he immediately seeks the cause and removes if, and that very largely is the kind and loving interest which is shown by the teachers in the Sunday school who watch over the spiritual growth of the children under their care, and by kindly advice direct their footsteps. And so we might elaborate the simile. ,To-day the fruits of the teachers' efforts are to be seen, and that they are good we .are all agreed. I am sure we are all delighted with the service this afternoon, and must congratulate those who have been responsible for the training of the voices and helped to make the effort such a success." . In the evening Mr. R. Silson again preached, the Mission being well filled. The Mission choir rendered two anthems in admirable style, con- ducted by Mr. A. Morris. Mr. W. Barton, sen,, presided at the organ. The collections realised £l3 10s.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

War Charity Funds.

Lady Emmott, who rendered valuable help tO the Belgian refugees in the early days of the war, is the only lady on the new committee for the control of war charity funds, which has recently been appointed by the Home Secretary. No fewer than eighty thousand Belgians fled to our shores during the autumn of 1914, the greater number of whom were utterly destitute. The problem of finding the right kind of hos• pitality for such a host of strangers was a .most difficult one, but Lady Emmott brought her skill and tact to bear upon the matter with the most successful results. She is an ardent Imperialist, and a prominent leader in all that concerns the progress and consolidation of the Empire, and is also actively connected with the Victoria League, in which she has been the leading spirit, from the beginning of that patriotic association. The Work of the Y.W.C.A.

The public is growing more than a trifle senti- mental over the woman munition worker, yet the unsatisfactory conditions of employment that the war has revealed, existed in the case of most women workers in times of peace. Then, however, no one except perhaps the Young Women's Christian Association troubled about them sufficiently to bring about reforms. Munition factories sprang up last year like mushrooms in a meadow, and in most cases the machinery and' organisation necessary to maintain in health the thousands of women who were drawn into this new labour market, were entirely lacking. Many of the workers are little girls, who have not yet put up their hair, and these, with the elder girls and women, had to leave their homes at 4 a.m., because there were no lodgings to be had near their work, and to walk across ploughed fields to the factories in all states of the weather. The Y.W.C.A., how- ever, came to the rescue, and opened canteens, restaurants, rest rooms and hostels. All these places are made as cheerful 'as possible, and they have proved of inestimable service to the thousands of women and girl workers who use them.

HOME NOTES.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

Food Values. Professor Long points out that an average man doing a fair amount of physical exercise requires each day sufficient food to provide 3,000 units of heat or energy. The following units per penny may be obtnined :—From the lean of beefsteak at Is. 6d. a pound, 60 units. Plaice at Bd. a pound, 15 units. Two- eggs, weighing four ounces, at 11d. each, or is. a pound, 50 units. - One pound of wholemeal bread, costing 2d., 600 units. An ounce of butter at 16d. a pound, 225 units. Margarinp at 6d. a pound, 600 units for a penny. Cheese at is. a pound, 180 units. Milk at 4d. a quart, 150 units. Rice at 2d. a pound, 800 units. Potatoes at id. a pound, 350 units. Haricot beans at 3d. a pound, 550 units. Sugar at 3d. a pound, 600 units. A rice pudding made with one quart of milk, five ounces of rice, and sugar sufficient to sweeten costs less than 51d., and is sufficient to provide nearly eight ounces of food for each of five People—containing 250 units of energy at the cost of a penny each.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
THE JOYLESS FACE.

No One Has Any Right To Inflict It On A Long-Suffering Public.

"My clear," said an experienced man to a girl who was about to start out in the world for herself, "you will have nutty hardships in the life ahead of you, but do not herald them in your face. No matter how blue You may be feeling, nor how bitterly things have gone awry—look cheerful! No one ha % a right to inflict a joyless face upon the public." That girl took "look cheerful" for her motto. She made a strenuous effort to look happy, and with the effort she declares that happiness seemed to follow. Her bright spirit, no matter what. went wrong, made her a popu- lar worker, who was always in line for advance- ment.

~..._ "Look cheerful" is a good motto for every

girl and woman to blazon over her mirror. It is not wise nor right to spoil the lives of others because things do not go our way. Without doubt you will have to work to seem gay—the tendency of human feelings being, almost .without exception, in the other direc- tion—and sometimes your efforts will fail, but keep up the fight and you will most surely cease to be a dejected, complaining individual whom no one wants around. Smile and you feel hopeful. There are some persons who luxuriate in woes. Each trifle that goes amiss is blazoned on their faces. The mouth gets a droop, and it hi as impossible for them to look content as for ice to thaw in zero weather. Be very sure if you are one of the down-in- the-mouth, life-is-a-grievous-blunder sort of a girl, no one will crave your society. Look cheerful, because it is poor business to look anything else, and do it in the interest of your own popularity.

Women Farm Labourers.

Over a hundred women have been trained for working on the land at the farms in Essex, lent by the Hon. Edward Strutt for that purpose. The women have taken up the work in a fine spirit of patriotism and devotion to their country. The Essex War Agricultural Com- mittee has a woman's section, presided over by Lady Petre., who announced at a meeting held at the end of April, that over two thousand women had volunteered as farm workers, but that when the returns of the registers were com- pleted, the number for Essex would be mucli higher. Young ladies who before the war were unable to dispense with the services of a French maid, have undertaken the roughest work on farms, which labourers' wives refused to do. They not only milked cows, but carted manure and cleaned out piggeries. A suitable dress, consisting of a blue or white overall, with breeches and leggings has been devised for these young farm workers, and a party thus attired last week drove a number of beasts to the cattle market held in the county town, after which lunch was partaken of in one of the principal hotels. Severe Tailor-mades.

The early summer tailor-made suits aid frocks have already made their appearance, and are chiefly remarkable for the combination of different fabrics, and for the sudden revival of the basque. Skirts are still short and full; coats may be long or short, but amplitude is the distinctive feature of garments of all descrip- tions. The ready-to-wear tailor-mades have had a great success this spring, for copies of the best and smartest French models have been offered at quite moderate prices. Many women spend the greater part of the day in tailored suits, which are severely simple in style; the cut of these garments is irreproachable, and the quality of the material used for them of the best. The newest models for summer wear show cape-like shoulder effects, which are in fact their most prominent features. Some f these capes are quite novel in shape, ending in points in front, when they reach the waist, and having one or two points behind. A con- trast in colour is effected by means of this cape. which may be of white cloth on a green or blue suit. There is no other trimming of any kind, and the coats—and frequently the capes also, are fastened by buttons covered with the cloth of which the garment is made.

Read This To Your Husband.

The eloquent Lyman Abbott, at an editors' luncheon in New York, advocated an allowance —as it were a salary—for the wife. Dr. Abbott concluded his strong argument with a cape in point. A man," he said," upbraided his helpmate for the extravagant cost of a new lingerie gown. " If you women had to earn your money,' he barked, you'd be a little more careful how you spent it.' "His wife laughed shrilly. "'Earn it indeed ! ' she cried. How would you men like to beg yours?"

Dresty Blouses. . The daintiest frocks in washing inatei!als will be popular this summer, but care must be exercised in the choic of materials and colours, or disappointment will result and expense be incurred in ren&wing faded gowns. The same remark applies to many of the new cotton blouses and shirts. The lingerie blouse, although much more expensive, always gives satisfaction to the wearer. If of fine materials and hand-worked, it wears and washes well, and the last preserves a certain exclusiveness that Is always attractive. Evening blouses get more beautiful every season, and their decoration (Ml 5 spring includes hand work in numerous furtm, Materials such as georgette, crepe, chirion and ninon are most beautifully embroider,o with silk picked out with coloured beads. Huge pipings and smockings, openwork stitching, delicate insertions of lace suggesting scrolls, loops, and bows, and many other devices are all requisitioned for this sefison's blouses. The newest models all show short full basques, but they are becoming only to the slimmest of fig7lre.

The Now Lingerie, Crepe de chine lingerie despite its tipparent extravagance, is steadily growing in favour, and gradually taking the place of lawn, nainsook, and line baptiste, probably because these materials Eire much scarcer; And consequently dearer than before the war. In the matter of crepe de chine it is decidedly the first step that costs, as when the material iias.beefi boty,x,ht, no further outlay is necessary, since ow effects in trimming can be obtained by MeiiiiSof here- stitching and picot edging. Lawn and tiain. sook are less expensive fabrics, but they moire fine laces as trimmings, and these are by 18e6 means cheap. Moreover, crepe de chine costs' less for laundering—a consideration in these days. Then one garment does duty for the three formerly made of cotton goods, and this new departure in underwear is really a very great saving in expense. The triple garment consists of camisole, knickers and petticoat, which is put on much the same as a coat. The envelope chemise is another time and material saving invention that is sure of success; it is so practical. The American vest too is simplicity itself. and is made of two straight pieces of crepe de chine with shoulder straps of the same, or of ribbon. Many women have lately adopted it, and are loud in its praise.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Pneumonia In Children.

Dr. R. G. Freeman regards pneumonia in children as a disease in housing. It has little tendency to develop in summer, and rarely occurs in winter among children kept in the open air. Open air he finds a more efficient remedy than any drug, serum, or vaccine. At the Roosevelt Hospital, New York, the othei conditions in children which Dr. Freeman has found most benefited by open air are tuber- culosis, amemia,' chorea, marasmus, and con- valescence from any disease. In pneumonia the routine treatment is to give an initial dose of castor oil, put the patients to bed on the roof (which is partially covered and enclosed on the north, east, and west sides), and keep their extremities warm by means of ample bedclothes and hot-water bottles. Very seldom is any stimulant or expectorant given.— "Lancet." Palpitation and Indigestion.

"Digestive disorders of one kind or another are a prolific cause of palpitation of the heart," says Dr. A. B. Olsen in May "Good Health." "Both acute and chronic dyspepsia, as well as nervous dyspepsia, favour palpitation, and par- ticularly flatulence. Indeed, flatulence alone with the accompanying abdominal distension is. sufficient to provoke an attack." In the course of an interview with Miss Margaret Wintringer (published in the May "Good Health ") on the conditions existing in America on the drink question, the following statement was made: "Experience has taught that when a city woman takes to drink, it always starts with the prescription of some doctor for a dose of port wine, brandy, or some other alcoholic beverage, but recently there has been something of a revolution among the doctors and the vast majority now rarely prescribe alcohol in any form to their patients." It is unwise to administer port wine, brandy, whisky, or any alcoholic beverages in the case of palpitation of the heart. for the alcohol is almost certain to aggravate the mischief. The mere act of sipping water is itself mild and effectual stimulant. But rest in a recumbent or reclin- ing position with several pillows to the head is necessary whatever other measures may be taken.—A. B. Olsen, M.D., in "Good-health."

WHAT A MUSSEL SHELL DID.

In an old record of Colonial times is a singular and true story which illustrates the endless chain of results which often depend on an in- significant action. In 1780 the son of a poor widow named Fulton was a pupil in a little country school in Lan- caster County, Pennsylvania. Anotheroboy who had seen a painter at work in Philadelphia made brushes for himself of hog's bristles and vege- table paints which he had kept in mussel shells. Young Fulton had never seen a painted picture, and he watched for months, with breathless in- terest, his schoolmate at work. At last the artist) in a sudden fit of generosity, gave him half the mussel shells and brushes. From that clay Fulton's whole heart was in his work of painting. His mother removed him to Philadelphia and bound him to a silversmith, but the boy, as soon as he was free, became a painter. With his first savings he went to England. A Quaker artist liked the boy and took him into his family. He there met Lord .Stanhope, a famous mechanician, whose influence inducel him to study mechanics. He entered the family of Joel Barlow, and there thoroughly educated himself as a civil engineer. His inventions in submarine navigation brought him both fame and money. In 1807 he launched in New York a steamboat, the proof of the great success of his life, the application of steam as a propelling power. '

- Between the mussel shell and the boat plough• ing its way up the river Hudson—wtat a vast space! Yet had the schoolmate been less gener- ous Robert Fultori might have lived and died a village labourer. The tinder and fuel were ready. It was no accident that supplied tha spark.

A good head and industrious hand are worth gold in any .land,.
