THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MAY 13, 1916.

" LABOUR " AND THE . PENSIONS COMMITTEE.

A special meeting of the Town Council will be held on Tuesday afternoon to appoint the members of the local committee 'under the Naval and Military War Pensions Act. A certain proportion of that committee has to be nominated by the representatives of Labour. It so happens that at the meeting of the representatives of Labour, called by the Mayor for the purpose of getting this pro- portion nominated for the final decision of the Council, a large sprinkling of members of the Trades and ,Labour Council were selected. We believe that the ,size of the proposed committee will allow of the appoint- ment of five Labour representatives. The Labour meeting chose six from which the Council might select the five. But of these six, four were Trades Council members; and it has been fairly well established that the Trades Council, as at present constituted, are a very long way from representing Labour in Warrington. The Trades Council have, through their chief members, not only abstained from assisting the national cause in the present troubles, but have offered that cause active opposition. In several instances powerful trades have seceded from the Coun- cil who still presume to speak for "Labour," because, we submit, the working men in most of the Unions have been content to allow cer- tain men to get themselves into positions of power. The Town Council will have to face the fact that, by whatever means, these names have been submitted, by those who profess to represent Labour. They will, on the other hand, have be bear in mind that the members of the committee that is to be formed will have to deal with the pensions of the men who have been out to fight the coun- try's battles on land and sea. As a corre- spondent pointed out in "The Examiner" recently, these returning heroes will resent having their claims reviewed by men who have stood "neutral," and glorified in their neutrality when their services have been de- sired, who became vigorous opponents of the suggestion that they with others should be compelled to do their share in saving the country that shelters them, and who, when positions on committees and tribunals came into being were most anxious to take a hand in that part of the game. The Town Council have to select five Labour men. As we have said, they have six from which to select. Whether it would be advisable for them to do more than carefully to select the one for rejec- tion and ask Labour to make another selec- tion is for themselves to say. It is a delicate situation, but the representatives of our "rotten borough" may be trusted to come to a wise 'decision on the facts placed before them.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY'S WILL-WON'T POLICY.

Germany's answer to America',, demand that piomisciious sinking of peaceable ships shall cease is a somewhat sorry affair. for a professedly logical-minded nation. It says that we began the inhumanities of the war, forgetting Louvain; and against such acts as the sinking- of the Lusitania it puts the many millions of women and children in Germany, who, "according to the avowed intention" of the British Government, are to be starved. Germany, however, evidently feeling herself in a tight corner; offeis the4,concession." that submarine commanders shall act in accordance with the principles of Interna- tional law, that is, that no ship. shall ie sunk without warning, and without saving the lives of those on board, unless the ship attempts to escape or to offer resistance. But having made the concession, she makes the impossible stipulation that America shall insist upon Great Britain abandoning our blockade methods, President Wilson has accepted the concession and has neglected the condition. Germany has to observe the concession she has made, in which case her submarine policy is robbed of most of its value to its inhuman she .must witness the breach of .kaumatic relations with the only great Ation of the world that is still OU friertdlT tow with her.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
GOSSIP.

The Education Committee have arranged to pay the Baths Committee .R 75 for the use of the baths—by scholars, of course, and not the committee during the approaching season.

Empire Day is next Wednesday week. The teachers have been requested to make "appro- priate references" in their respective schools.

"It is considered a luxury to put straw fodder under a horse," declared .a speaker at a meet- ing of the Warrington and District War Agri- cultural Committee on Wednesday night. This great scarcity is owing to our " Tommies" hav- ing taken to stables. We are also told that at the Front they work like horses.

A few weeks ago there was an appeal, signed by several bishops, scientists, politicians, and other public-spirited gentlemen, asking us to abstain from meat on at least one day in seven.

"It has made no difference at all," was the purport of the remarks of several Warrington butchers to me yesterday. Another sermon gone West! Yet it is highly important that we should conserve our meat supply.

A friend of mine has been much perturbed this week by learning that newly-born, lambs have been immediately hurried to Warrington, sold, killed, and offered to the public for con- sumption.

I was interested to notice that Mr. Howard was present at the Trades and Labour Council meet- ing on Tuesday. Last month, I believe, he was taking a holiday in the hills of Scotland.

The Trades Council clock was ten minutes fast, and somedne stood on a chair (the orange box for Bridge Foot outdoor meetings being in disrepair) to put it back. ." Someone will have to put it forward an hour at the next meeting," remarked Mr. Dukes. Glad they are going to keep abreast with the times!

"I'm not going to put my clock on for any- body," said an old gentleman in my hearing in a hairdresser's shop. "What's the good of altering the clock? The Government is always too late, and now they are trying to make up time by putting on the clock. Ugh! They're not going to get me to do it." Luckily, the old gentleman has not got to answer a buzzer in a morning, so he may please himself what he does. There is no danger of him losing "a quarter."

A prisoner at the Borough Police Court on Monday called a couple of policemen "two evil spirits." The prisoner himself looked ghostly on being sentenced to a month's hard labour.

"I have a fashion of talking to myself when I have no one else to talk to," said another pris- oner. Does he ever feel bored with himself?

At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday a policeman emphasised the seriousness of a lighting offence by pointing out that the gas illuminated a house across the road. "What! Warrington gas?" inquired Mr. Henry Greenall ever so insinuatingly.

My friend and office colleague, Sydney Jeffery, now on active service, has an article in the May Number of "The Dickensian" on "Dickens and Peace." lam longing for the days when " peace " will have arrived and Mr. Jeffery will be writing again in "The Examiner" his learned disquisitions on "Dickens "—and other subjects.

Have you made a start yet towards getting some of the sovereigns for 15s. 6d.? If not, why not?

We have all heard of the family man who enlisted and went to the war to get a bit of peace. Another phase of domestic difficulty was 'revealed at Harrow on Wednesday, when the Tribunal excused a man for "exceptional "domestic circumstances." He said his wife had run away with the lodger and left him to look after five children.

The harsh weather is delaying the work of Nature in the fields and gardens. The lilac seems to be uncertain about forgiving the frosts and cold winds that have afflicted it since it was on the point of blooming prema- turely in January.

Nature is always threatening the peaches, the wheal, the corn and the health, but on the average it does pretty well.

"The planting of trees in streets and public spaces is a sure means of increasing the health- fulness of a town, as well as adding to ita beauty."

That is an opinion expressed before Zeppelins were invented. Now, with our darkened streets, trees, like lamp posts and tram standards, are a sure means, if we are not very careful, of sending us to the doctor with a bruised fore- head or broken nose—a kind of beauty only somewhat misplaced.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR AMBULANCE.

Red Cross Society Appeal For

£403.

Miss Sylvia Broadbent, vice-president of the Daresbury Division Cheshire Branch of the British Red Cross Society, writes:— "When an appeal for a motor ambulance was made, a short time ago, it was with the idea of sendinc, one to the Front. Ambulances are still needed there, but it appears that there is a still more urgent need for one locally. "When a hospital train comes into Warring- ton, much of the transport has to be sent from Manchester, which entails waste in the running or the cars. The subscribers to the ambulance fund are willing that the money should be used in whichever way seems best. "I send quotations from a letter from the Chairman of the Transport Committee of the Cheshire 8.R.C.5., in answer to various ques- tions of mine, from which it will be seen that a motor ambulance stationed at Warrington will be of great use; and it is agreed by the B.R.C.S. that it shall be retained there after the war.

"It will be the gift of the Daresbury Division, with which Warrington is amalgamated. Already more than half of the required .2400 has been received, and it is hoped that the re- mainder will be contributed as soon as possible, so that the ambulance may be obtained without loss of time."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
Local Needs.

The Chairman of the Cheshire Branch writes: There is undoubt:dly urgent need for an ambulance in your. district. The ambulance can be kept at Warrington, primarily for local use, but we should, if it is provided, ask permission to use it elsewhere upon emergency.

£4OO would certainly cover the cost and pro- vide spare parts and stores. If you care to leave the matter in my hands, I can promise the subscribers excellent value for their money. The County Secretary adds that there will be no objection td the ambulance being retained after the war for local use, and that can be made one of the stipulations of the gift.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Death of Mr. David Hodue. •

We regret to announce the death of Mr. David Ritchie Hddge, of 211, Wilderspool-causeway, who passed away with painful suddenness on Wednesday evening.. Mr. Hodge was a native of Scotland, and the first part of his business career was spent as a manufacturing cabinet- maker. He came to Warrington as a depart- mental manager for Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, and had been fifteen years in their service. He was widely known in Warrington, and his death will be regretted by a large number of friends. Mr. Hodge had entered his seventy-first year. The funeral takes place to-day at three o'clock at the Warrington Cemetery.

Case of Necessity. Boyd is a bright little fellow of three years. One day his mother observed him vigorously scratching his head and asked: "What makes you scratch your head?' "Because I'm the only one that knows it itches," he replied quickly.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 13, 1916.

WALKING DAY.

Children's Festival To Be Held On June 30th.

ALL SCHOOLS UNITED.

Thousands of children ins.Warrington will be almost overjoyed to know that this year Walk- ing Day will be observed. June 30th is the date fixed.

Last year this 'great annual festival was allowed to lapse, as far as the town as a united whole was concerned, each Sunday school making its own arrangements and working in unfortunate isolation. There was little pageantry and no adorning of the Town Hall lawn with merry children. The Mayor (Alder- man , Peter Peacock) and other prominent 'townsmen certainly took a great interest in the separate Processions but the grand march past the Town Hall steps was a missing link in a festival which should have a happy and picturesque chain of events, despite the fact that we were at war. With no town backing, therefore, Sunday school officers, who foresaw ttemendous disappointment for the children, made their own .plans for its diminution. Result: emasculated processions ingloriously wending their way to neighbouring fields on the Saturday afternoon and a few schools postpon- -ing lessons next day in order that the children inight 'parade their parishes.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIEP,S WILL BE PLEASED.

All this, we know, was due_ to the war. Out of consideration for our boys in the trenches the municipal authorities felt a festival would bt: out of harmony, but they have since learned that besides their "laissez-faire" attitude being regretted by the children, it was deplored by those at the Front. These brave men feel that they are "out there" to protect us, our homes, and our enjoyments, and when they hear that Walking Day is to be. held this year in the dear old way they will be among the heartiest supporters. They want the children in any case to be happy. At a meeting of the clergy and representa- tives of Sunday schools convened by the Mayor at the Town Hall on Monday, it was decided to resume the festival.

His Worship presided and, in his opening statement, admitted that a great disappoint- ment was caused to the .children last Year by not holding Walking Day, and as there was some danger of the festival being discontinuea if it were not held this year, oe thought that Walking Day should be resumed. He also stated that last year he received several letters from the Front .expressing disappointnurat that it bad been decided not to hold the festiva' as usual. His Worship further suggested that the superintendents of those schools which had been in the habit of going out of town might consider the desirability of remaining in town.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
AGREEMENT ARRIVED AT.

After some discussion in favour of holding the day as usual, or' of following last year's arrangements, the following resolution was Passed : "That Walking Day be held on Friday, the 30th June, but that having regard to the need for economy at the present time, the display be modified; that the whole of the religious bodies in the town join in th procession, and that the children should assemble in the afternoon in fields in the - town or in the immediate nei,;hbrmrhood, for the purpose of sports, It was suggested that there should be no flowers, and with a view to economising in labour each school should have only one bsn- ner and no statues should be curled in the procession.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
SLIGHTLY MODIFIED.

Of course, this will rob the Day of much of its picturesqueness, but for it to be otherwise would .have required ,more labour than would' probably be forthcoming. The wonderful pro- cessions which the Roman Catholics are accus- tomed to are not formed u ithout long and painstaking effort. For weeks arrangements are in hand, and now that the works are going at such pressure it is doubtful whether the men could be spared to carry the banners and statues usuallY displayed. For the eiglat mag- nificent harmers customarily shown by the St. Mary's (Thattermarket-street) R.C. no fewer than forty men are required, these acting as transporters in relays. We may be sure, how- ever, that despite the simplicity of this year's show, the children will look charming and happy. It is probable that iAtead of the denomina- tions assembling in front of the Town Hall at varying times, they will con,r'reate simultan- eously and sing the National Anthem. His Worship has promised to endeavour to secure the services of the Blue Coat School Band and the bands of the Boy Scouts and other similar organisations.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
MAJOR CROSFIELD, D.S.O.

Latest Report 3 Record A Steady

Recovery.

We are informed that Major Crosfield con- tinues to gain ground steadily, and that accord- ing to the latest reports has completely got the mastery of the septic poisoning. Indeed, it seems not improbable that another fortnight, or even less, may see him back again on the soil of the Old Country.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
FOUND OWN HOMES.

Bekjians Remove After Hospitality Of

Wesleyans.

The Wesleyan Circuit Bible- Class Belgian Home in Palmyra-square is tha subject of an interesting report by Mr. Charles E. Parker in this month's "Church Record," in the course of which he describes the human susceptibilities of the refugees. ' "The highest number reached in the home was 16," he states. "Over the whole periof.l accommodation was given to seven Belgian families, one of which consisted of a woman and three daughters, who were only able to contribute a trifle toward their maintenance. Various changes in the home were made during the year, some of the inmates finding more con- genial work in other towns. All came to the home in a depressed and impoverished condition, some of them having undergone great hardships before reaching this land of safety. All their home belongings were lost, they were depressed in mind and overwrought in body. As the male members got to work and the families got settled they soon showed signs of improvement. This was manifested in various ways, one being that the different families quarrelled amongst each other, probably just as we English would have done under similar circumstances. One of them remarked that 'they could get on very well with the English people, but it was very difficult to agree with the Belgians.' "At first the committee allowed the male members to retain their earnings until such time as they were able to reclothe themselves, after- wards they were made to contribute to their own maintenance. The various families have been set upon_their feet and are now support- ing themselves by their earnings in Warrington and elsewhere. As there were no more Belgians requiring homes it was decided to give up the home at the end of March, 1916.

"The committee and the supporters have good reason to be satisfied with the part they have taken in helping these unfortunates, and our guests have shown and often expressed their appreciation of the kindness given to them." The cash statement shows that the Bible Classes contributed £146 7s. 3d. and Mr. C. E. Parker £5O.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
SHAKESPEARE'S PHILOSOPHY.

" This Is God's World "—The

Keynote Of His Books.

THE REV. T. T. JAMES' LITERARY SERMON.

Always up-to-date, interesting, and inspiriting, the Rev. T. T. James, at Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church on Sunday morning, delivered a notable discourse on Shakespeare. "Some may be asking why we should have a sermon on Shakespeare at all," he said. "Well, firSt of all, because his tercentenary is an event in our national life. It is not unfitting that it should be so, for no one had more of the love of country than this poet of the Elizabethan England, the England with its eyes on two new worlds, one discovered by Columbus and the other by Copernicus, With its first beginnings of Colonial Empire and its glowing belief in its own greatness and its own future. Not even the war has been Able to prevent it being to a great ex- tent an international event—though it would have been far more that but for this conflict— and it demands attention. We are to regard such stupendous gifts of genius as Shakespeare possessed as one of God's great gifts to the world, and a gift used honourably and bravely so that the world is and will be the better for it. Master Of Human Problems.

"Above and beyond everything else in William Shakespeare's work there is the mastery of the problems and passions of human life, the power to see into the elemental and per- manent emotions of the soul, the portrayal of the ambitions and aspirations, the hope and fear, the joy and tragedy of human experience. This, of course, is the thing that makes lasting literature, the secret which commands filo at- tention age after age. It is just this same secret which makes much of the literature of the Bible a vital and permanent thing: the Psalms are indestructible because they have the univer- sal note which goes beneath all outward and, changing forms of social organisation and time conditions to the universal and eternal elements of human experience. So with our great Eng- lish dramatist we feel that we are in the pre- sence of one who knew human nature, and in ' the mystery of all those struggles Qf the soul which make up this strange and interesting life of ours, his is the power which can fill in the colours and make the picture real. This was a secret learned, as far as 'one can say it was learned at all and not the result of natural genius, in the school of actual experience more than in the world of book knowledge. The end- less conflict between good and evil in the human heart, the vivid setting of the interplay of emotion and passion, the acute analysis of the inward and unseen realities, these placed for us in outward scenes of history, or tragedy, or comedy, are the things which make Shakespeare perennial in his appeal. And now I want to go further than that and ask whether beneath all this wonderful representation it is possible to discover some kind of philosophy of life? I do not mean to ask whether Shakespeare set out to preach some definite gospel, for I do not think he did. It is probably true to say that as a dramatist he felt it to be his task to represent character in action, and that he succeeded in it to a degree of superlative success. But as in his drama he presented character in action, do we in the end find certain principles present- ing themselves, certain truths being clearly stated, certain ideals being unmistakably held up? Of course, when we sit down to read these plays we are conscious at once that varied and contradictory systems of life are being presented to us, because they were varied and contra- dictory characters which had to be drawn. Do we get up at the finish feeling that through the clash and play of individual and event certain truths are being brought home by which it will be well for men to live? I think we do.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
God's World.

"It is impossible not to feel that this is God's world we live in. Tragic often enough ay be the scenes through which we are taken, rough and boisterous may be the scenes of human love and hate, strange and startling the vicissi- tudes of history, but above and beyond it all, above and beyond the conflicts and strifes of men, and the passion and change of human life, you come again and again .upon the idea of the over-ruling PrOVidence and Power.- History at last, however much it may be man's making, is in the hands of God.

'0 God, Thy arm was here, And not to us, but to Thy arm alone Ascribe we all.' And if that be true, equally true it is that w4n life is over to God we go at last. The light of immortality is shining. . . . The idea of God as in the end the supreme controller of all things rises and confronts us everywhere. The real things are ultimately the unseen things. Argument and discussion interminable have arisen over Shakespeare's use of the super- natural, and it is possible to see in it only a kind of blind following of a survival of medire- valism. But there is something, too, of the true mystic about the supernatural in the Midsum- mer Night's Dream' and in 'The Tempest.' The desert island is alive with mystic presences and eloquent with mystic voices. . • . Is it fanciful to suppose that Shakespeare was saying to us that after all the most real things are not those that we can see; that the spiritual mean- ing of the universe is the last word about it, and that men and women, and things material, are but the shadows of the things that are to come? At any rate, you cannot read him with- out feeling that in the little lives of men God has to be reckoned with, and that Ile is in the place of supremacy. "You leave your reading of Shakespeare with the idea that there has, been set in front of you the validity of the conventional virtues. If anyone seeks a writer who, will hold up to ridicule the simple faith of men in the every- day virtues of honesty. and ,purity of heart ani mercy and forgiveness he must seek him else- where than here. Doubtless the poet had a wide tolerance with the weaknesses and frailties of the human heart, for he knew its nature intimately, but his ethical teaching is that duty fulfilled is the only path to happiness. It is impossible to trample on the homely honesties of life without incurring punishment and pain and retribution. The fires of remorse for sin have never been painted in more realistic fashion than in the picture of Lady Macbeth, a wilful sinning woman with a heart stressed by terror now that the crime is committed. . . .

===== 0002601_19160513_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
Punishment Follows Sin.

"Shakespeare tells us that the end of sin is punishment, and the way to happiness is by virtue, and the pursuit and acquisition of it is at last largely independent of circumstance and environment,. it; is a matter of the individual and inward life. Will and character are the things that count, and a man is not the spbrt of fate. The poet has little or no patience with that modern spirit -which sees man as merely the result of certain inherited tendencies played upon, by outward forces. He believes in a man- hood which faces facts and conquers. 'Are these things then necessities? Then let us meet them like necessities.' And so, beneath or within the magic mastery •which presents the passion:and the play of life, we find ourselves face to face with moral and spiritual' philosophy; we come to the great verities of God in history and in individual life, of 'man's moral 'responsibility for what he is and does, of virtue as the path of happiness, of duty as the end of life. This is one of God's gifts to us; this man of genius who makes these things so plain. 'Whatsoever things are honest, true, just', pure, lbvely, of good report; if there be any virtue, and if there be any praise, think on these things.'" •

THEEOMINOUS QUESTION.

Trades And Labour Council Perturbed By Coun. Roberts.

DUKES' OPEN-DOOR TO CRITICS.

That the Trades and Labour Council is somewhat perturbed by a question addressed to the Mayor by Councillor Henry Roberts respecting the composition of the Army and Naval Pensions Committee, was manifested at the monthly meeting, held: in the Socialist Hall, on Tuesday night. It will be remembered that at last week's Town Council meeting the Mayor, in reply to Councillor Roberts, declared that the Town Council is the deciding body as to the person- nel of the new committee and had the power of refusing to adopt the names of those who were nominated if it were thought fit. It so happens that at a recent conference of trade union officials six of those nominated for the committee are prominent members of the Trades 'Council, these being Messrs. Dukes, Plinston, Seabury, and Roole, the others re- commended being Messrs. Baker and Hodson. The following discussion took place at Tues- day's meeting of the •Trades and Labour Council.

Mr. Shimmins (addressing the chair) : Have you seen the question raised by Councillor Henry Roberts at the Town Council meeting? To my mind there is something underlying that question, and I think we ought to take some action here to-night to go against that if anything is done in the matter.

liamblett : It was noted at our executive meeting. We have our eyes on the matter. Mr. Dukes: I do not happen to have the Press-cutting by me, but we certainly noted it. I 'do not think we are altogether wise in anticipating what it may contain or mean. It may have reference to other bodies than this, but if it has not I suggest that we wait until they deal with it. I think we can safely assume that we shall be able to meet any criticism, if it is levelled, against the repre- sentatives of labour. But the most that you can do now is to suggest that your executive meet and a special council be called including the representatives of those unions which are not affiliated with the Council. As a matter of fact, as far as I am concerned, I would open the doors to those who have an objection to us. The meeting would certainly be inter- esting.

The Chairman (Mr. E. Plinston) : I think we had better let the matter lie for the present. We who were present at that meeting know that the position was made very, very clear, and it was agreed upon by those present— some of whom have nothing to do with the Council at all—that those nominated should be strongly recommended to the Town Council in the order of procedence declared by the number of votes given to each. We had the assurance of the Mayor, Alderman James Evans and Councillor Lindley that that would be done. If the wish of the Town Council is to ride rough-shod over the names of those nominated then we must do something worthy of the Trades Council, which is not yet defunct. (Hear, hear.)

The Chairman's advice was regarded as being wise and was acquiesced in by the Council.

BOOTS FOR RUSSIANS.

9,009,000 Pairs To Be Made In This Country.

The British Army Contract Department are now making rapid progress with the details regarding the 9,000,000 pairs of boots of several types which are required from British boot and shoe manufacturers to meet the needs of the Russian Army. This order is by far the largest ever undertaken in this country, and the magnitude of the task will be grasped when it is stated that 3,000,000 pairs are required for delivery before the end of June next. That is to say, that in less than nine full weeks there has to be an output of no less than over 333,000 every week between now and the date on which they must be completed and delivered to the British Government. To assemble the various kinds of leather required to produce on an average more than 55,000 pairs of boots per day involves very great skill and complete organ- isation, in order that all the machinery may be kept working up to its full capacity. The details of•the order have produced a very great sensation. The boots are of the weights and sizes for giants, and the Russian troops for whom they are intended must be men of magni- ficent physique. The sizes run from 10's to 15's, and 71b. to 81b. the pair are quite common weights.—" Yorkshire Post."

The Warrington correspondent of the "Leather Trades Review," writing of the Russian order, says "The Government have at last settled the question of terms, and notices were in the hands of the tanners on Friday last stating that all leather suitable for this order may be pro- ceeded with at once, and buyers who have been allocated quantities must give a written guar- antee stating that it will be used solely for this contract, otherwise no business will be enter- tained from the tanner's point of view. Un- fortunately this business has caused some un- pleasantness among many of the leading factors who attended the deputation to the Govern- ment, for I understand some strongly urged that 2 per cent. was insufficient, but others were quite agreeable to carry the business through on the above terms. However, the matter is now finally settled, and we hope to get on with the business as fast as possible. Trade during the past week has been brisk. Buyers have been plentiful in this district, and leather in all grades, not suitable for the Russian order, have been readily snapped up at advanced prices; in fact, many seeking leather for the repairing trade have gone away empty-handed, and I•do not see how things are going to im- prove unless the Government prevent further dilution of labour in the yards."

A Week's Deaths.

During the week ended May 6 there were 20 deaths in Warrington against 21 in the corre- sponding week last year. The causes were: Influenza, 3; heart disease, 3; phthisis, 2; other tubercular disease, 1; premature birth, 2; bron- chitis, 1; accident, 1; erysipelas, 1; whooping cough, 1; all other causes, 5.

The Pound Day at Raddon Court 11,jspital was held on Wednesday, and many useful and acceptable gifts, including eggs, groceries, and money were brought. Tea was served in the hall, and the patients there drew for a water- colour picture, painted by Mrs. Wm. Fletcher, and raffled by her for the Whitley Red Cross Working Party. The winning number was 23, and the picture was won by Miss Wooc, Thorneyholme, Old Trafford. A short enter- tainment was given and was much enjoyed by the visitors and patients.

Attested Married Men in Conference.

The National Union of Attested Married Men have met twice in Warrington during the past week, on Sunday, hearing an interesting speech from Mr. Meadowcroft, who was one of a recent deputation to the Prime Minister. Regarding the future policy of the union it was stated that a scheme was in hand for the formation of advisory committees in every centre to watch the interests of the wives and children of those who will be called to the Colours. it is 'con- sidered that such committees may be of con- siderable service in the presentation of claims for financial relief when the new Tribunals, which have to administer the Government scheme, are set up. The union are appealing for the interest of all married men of military age, whether attested or not, as, of course, it is of far-reaching importance.

Wycliffe P.S.A.'s Tribute to Fallen Soldiers.

There was a good meeting on Sunday, presided over by Mr. B. Farrar. Gardner, of 5, Worsley-street. To the parents and family of Arthur Payne, also to Mrs. H. Gardner and family, and their near ones there was sent a resolution of deepest sympathy. To ,Sergeant T. Warrington Journalist's Appointment.

An announcement of interest to Warrington appeared in the "Newspaper World's" last issue—that Mr. Ernest H. Prince was recently formally appointed chief of the editorial staff of the "Liverpool Echo," for whose pages he has been mainly responsible for a long time. Mr. Prince began his journalism on the "War- rington Guardian," receiving his training under the present editor of "The Warrington Ex- aminer." He left Warrington to join the staff of the "Manchester Evening Chronicle," taking charge of their news telephones, and was soon appointed to the reporting staff, and then made a sub-editor. He was next given the assistant editorship of the "Sunday Chronicle," and after two years in that position resigned it to join the "Liverpool Echo," of which he is now in charge. Mr. Prince is married to a daughter of Mr. James Hooton, of Liverpool and Wal- lasey, and lives on Mersey'side. It is interesting that both the chief publisher of the "Echo" and his first assistant are old Warrington newspaper workers. The Seven Words from the Cross.

The Rev. W. Hendy Cock, vice-principal of the Warrington Training College, has issued by Mr. Robert Scott, Paternoster-row, E.C., a little book "Thoughts on the Seven Words from the Cross." It has been arranged on an interesting plan. A separate chapter is devoted to each of the seven words from the Cross, and the whole is preceded by an introduction. The purpose of the writer is to state his own ideas simply and in a few words, and having done that to depend for illustration and elaboration upon selections from the poets. The work has been well done, and the result is a stimulating volume. The work of a wide variety of poets has been called upon, and almost all the selections are very happily made. The chief field has been found in the English poets of the nineteenth century. That Tennyson and Browning should hold a considerable place is, of course, both desirable and inevitable, but minor poets of the period have not been forgotten. It may in- deed be put down as one of the services rendered by Mr. Cock that he has reminded us again of the writing of Arthur Hugh Clough, Lord Houghton, and Henry Kirke White. He has at the same time given us pages in which clear thought and spiritual insight go together. A Year of Easter Sundays. The Rev. IL B. Firth, writing in this month's "St. James' Magazine," said : "First let me say how extremely gratifying was the solemn observance of Good Friday. No such congregation, since I have been Vicar, has ever assembled in St. James' Church, and con- sidering the large number of our young men who are absent on military duty this is the more remarkable. All our people made a special effort to be present at the chief service of the day, and they must have induced many of their friends to accompany them. The celebration of Easter, too, was of an encouraging nature; the church was beautifully decorated, the musical parts of the services were nicely rendered, and, hest of all, there were large numbers of com- municants at all the celebrations, and the con- gregations at mattins and evensong were much above the average. How good it would be, and it is within the region of possibility, if the re- curring Sundays of the year were kept as an Ea:ster Day' in every week. Everyone enjoys the Easter services, and their popularity is largely due to the fact that they are so well attended. People love fellowship; there is something inspiring in the comradeship of reli- gion, and if our people would but flock to their churches in great numbers Sunday by Sunday, and 'walk in the House of God as friends,' how much more social and enjoyable our public worship would 'become."

THE BEER BOYOOTT!

Working Men's Protest To The

Licensed Victuallers.

NO PROSPECT OF A REDUCTION.

There ha S been less beer consumed in War- rington in the past fortnight than ever before during a similar period, this being due to a strike of consumers owing to increased prices. Every inn where the new prices are in vogue has had fewer merry men, walking into their bars, but where the prices have not been raised great business has been done. It is said that at one place a long queue restlessly waited some time until the rush indoors eased off. _

To the licensees demanding more money many protests have been sent by their one-time patrons, but all in vain. As many as sixty patrons of one house have signed a declaration that until the prices are lowered they will not enter the premises, the penalty of defection being a fine of 2s. 6d. That the higher prices have had a beneficial effect may be gathered from the remark of an ironworker: "Our forge has been more sober this last fortnight than I have ever known it, and I have worked in it for over twenty years." That there is no likelihood of a reduction was emphasised by a prominent licensed victualler in conversation with an " Examiner " interviewer last evening. "We do not mind less drinking," he said, "for the Government have issued a regulation that less beer has to be brewed, and now that the demand is lessening the problem of supply is being solved of itself. If the demand had not slackened we should eventually have been compelled to refuse customers. You may take it from me that those houses where the old prices still rule will soon have to raise them or refuse to sell more than a certain quan- tity. The Government will not allow them to cater for an increased demand, in fact, all breweries have got to manufacture less."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
FARMERS' GRIEVANCE.

Protest Against War Office

Restrictions.

The embargo placed on the sale and removal of hay, straw, &c., by the War Office was re- ferred to at a meeting of the Warrington and District War Agricultural Committee held at the Griffin Hotel, Warrington, on Wednesday night. The new restrictions seriously affect the position of farmers in Warrington and district. It was explained that farmers must not take to distributing hay, straw, &e., in order to defeat the -Defence of the Realm Act. The military apthorities had laid down a rule that where a town had not got a hay market stand, that town was not considered a hay market town. Warrington was not considered in that category, but Liverpool, Manchester,Wigan Preston and Southport were. They had taken a ten-mile area around these towns. If a War- rington and district farmer wanted to bring a load into Warrington he had to prove to the satisfaction of the military representatives that he had done so for three months, and he would have to secure a permit. A dealer could go to the farmer, offer £6 6s. per ton, and take the commodity away. It was further stated that permits could be obtained for wheat straw, but not for oat straw - Oat straw could not be sold. Several members called attention to the fact that no inquiries had been made respecting the quantity of straw in their possession, while others stated that straw taken over by the War Office had not yet been removed from the farms. It was pointed out that the War Office had given an undertaking to remove all hay and straw by August 14th next. It was resolved that the committee, through the County Agricultural Committee, and the Forage Committee, offer a most vigorous pro- test against the present unfair conditions i 231- posed upon farmers of this paiticular area. While the farmers residing within 10 miles' of a recognised hay market town have (under certain defined conditions) been allowed the privilege of supplying the market and their private customers according to the average quantities delivered prior to this order, the farmers of this particular locality are totalli debarred from selling as retailers, and sit tr market prices obtainable, inasmuch as t.','2l majority (if not all) live outside the 10 mile limit of any recognised hay market town. To remedy these grievances this ctraraitfeef advocates that the privileges as to the sale ° fodder in the open markets now permitted dealers, be extended also to the farmers— actual producers.

TEETOTAL BEER.

Exprimental Canteen At The Dallam And Bewsey Forges.

THIRSTY PUDDLERS.

Innumerable workmen at Dallam and Bewsay Forges have added to their drinking experiences this week, for on Monday Messrs. B. Cunning- ham opened a canteen there to supply non- alcoholic beer. In the trade a bottle of beer—or more—is regarded as almost an indispensable part of an ironworker's equipment, and the directors of P. and K. may be congratulated on being, as far as we are aware, the first local Company to give the recommendation of the Central Liquor Board a sound trial. In cases Where the consuming of beer on a by no means minor scale is a fixed habit the Board has advo- cated the substitution of non-alcoholic for the ordinary beer, maintaining that while satisfy- ing the thirst, it will also be powerless in the lessening of afficiency. In the new beer not more than two per cent. alcoholic gravity must he registered; otherwise, it is no longer "beer- -1058," and comes within the scope of the licensing authority. The new canteen has been the prime subject of Interest at P. and K. this week. "Have you been and Wad some yet" has been the cheery question put many times while "drawing a heat." - In selling hours the non-alcoholic beer has the advantage of the ordinary, as the canteen is permitted to remain open all through the day and night. The barmen. who work in shifts like the workmen. have had a busy time in replenish- Ing the cans held out to he refilled. Four or five barrels stand on the stills in tapping order, and the men have the pleasure of seeing their beer " drawn from the wood," ard th.n have the satisfaction of miffing the froth, which ceems to give tone and nietrty.ocnner.p.cc to the drink.

DEATH OF EX-ORFORD BARRACKS OFFICER.

THE TRIUMPHANT THREE.

Three fair young lives, scarce past the budding leaf, Have felt the pruning hook and yielded up their souls; Three mothers' sons have left the family sheaf, And garnered been to satisfy the Ghouls. Row long we watelied them,, each in divers ways, , Progress from stage to stage from Reason's birth., - Each just a human unit in the days When unit humans were accounted some- thing worth.

What' pride we felt in each unfolding mind! What joy we had in each expanding grace! What hope was ours that each an aim would find! And what content when each had found his place!

Then from our ken they passed, each in his turn, Into another school, the great world-school of strife, Where stranger lessons should be theirs to learn, To steel them for the battle-ground of life. Anon, we heard of them; this "doing grand," That "grown up fine," the other "learned his trade." Anon, we met them, gripped their growing hand, So changed, they almost men had made.

And then the war's alarms and bugle's blast That told of savage deeds to be ill done; Of nations °itr whom the gods had cast A spell of madness; and a blood-ringed sun. Soon the shrill shriek, "Your country needs You," reached the lads we all had known, And into full-fruit leaped the seeds Of Loyalty we once had sown. No pause for them to question "how" or "why" Our native land in danger hourly stood; Nor did they ask, "What voice had I In raising this foul welt of blood, Why should I leave my home and friends To fight for Statesmen's wicked crime, Why should I die—when dying ends My young life ere it's prime?"

No craven fear held them in chain, They went at Freedom's call; Their all they gave, none could remain If doubt of Honour's fall. These home-bred lads, these sons of kindly folk, Whose hearts had never burned To wound or slay, nor felt the yoke Of Tyrant's power, now grimly spurned To save themselves by ofrring mean excuse Which meaner men had thought. They thoughtless went, nor word of blame, abuse, Flung they at those who mean excuse had brought.

A tense farewell, a mother's last embrace, A father's word of strength, a comrade's toast, A short, rough training, then the word to face The armies of the mighty Teuton host.

In the mud trench, made Hell by cannons' crash, In slow advance, and sometimes in retreat; In shock of arms and driving bay'net clash In touch with bloody death; defeat They knew not, met it not, though pressed By hordes of men, of slaves To the great War Lord, who, by greed obsessed, Swept them to slaughter in unceaseless waves.

But in God's hour, on human Calv'ry Cross, These dear young souls made supreme Sacrifice, Yield all was gain, that never Death, was loss That wins and keeps eternal Paradise. No gloom bell tolls, No dirge is chanted here; These three young souls Have triumphed over bier; Weep not for them, God keeps them in His rest, Thank Him for them, He knew—and loved them best. W. M.

Employers have an important duty imposed 11,1)cm then' by a Defence of the Realm regula- *lion, which was made public in the "London Gazette" on Wednesday. Every employer must Prepare and post up a list of the male persons Whom he employs between 18 and 41, and ,nrust revise it at least once a month. Failure T.O Comply with this order will render them guilty of an offence.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 13, 1916.

LIBERAL AGENT VOLUNTEERS.

Preach.

MR. E. A. SANDERS, WITH MRS.

Mr. Ernest A. Sanders, the Liberal Agent for Warrington, took an active part in all the recruiting efforts that were made last year; and, under the Derby Scheme, there was not a more active worker than he in that great scheme to save the Voluntary System in Eng- land and to put an adequate, and therefore winning, Army in the field. Mr. Sanders was the means of inducing a large body of men to enlist, and now compulsion has come into being and persuasion has died a natural death, he is no longer required by the recruiting officers. He has, however, practising at the first possible moment what he has so successfully preached, offered his services to the War Office. That offer has been accepted; he has become a soldier of the King and will shortly be in military harness as a member of the supply branch of the Army Service Corps. Mr. Sanders is well over military age, but the War Office has been induced, in the special circumstances, to , waive that point; and we believe that Mr. Sanders is the only Warring- tonian who has enlisted as an over-aged recruit. There is, of course, very little doing in a political sense, and Mr. Sanders's work for the Liberal Association, as such, has dwindled very much. Still there was sufficient work to be done in connection with the various movements for raising funds and rendering services for our soldiers and sailors to keep the agent well occupied; but be desired. to get into closer touch with the real work of the war and so volunteered. The Finance Committee of the Liberal Association very generously released him and decided to augment the weekly allowance to his wife and child. Councillor John Wilkin- son (chairman) and Councillor J. T. Locker

COLONEL FAIRCLOUGH'S

GRIM HUMOUR.

The Mystery Of The Well.

Colonel Fairclough, commandinc, the local Territorials, writing on May 2nd 'to the Town Clerk, says:— "Dear Mr. Whittle,—Many thanks for the cigarettes and plug tobacco so kindly sent to the Battalion. The consignment is now dis- tributed and thoroughly. enjoyed. "The weather seems to have settled now and thoroughly hot sun is experienced all day. It is a most welcome change from the cold and wet which we had since about the end of October. The country is changing very rapidly now from winter bareness to summer's radiant clothing.. Swallows arrived on April 25th in large flocks, a few arriving late on the 24th. I heard the nightingale on the 27th. Magpies have been nesting for some time. Beeches are in full flower, and oak trees are in the stage when the young leaf has a bronze-like hue. Pear and cherry trees are just at the height of their bloom; apple-tree flowers have just opened from the bud stage. "The other day I saw the orange-tip butterfly and later the sulphur one; the purple emperor is very common.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
Preparing the Harvests.

"Grass for bay is about eight inches long, wheat is about one inch taller. Potatoes are being set, the whole of the available family being employed. Grandfather ploughs, grand- mother and daughters drop the potatoes into the furrow, young grandson and granddaughter carry potatoes from the sacks to those dropping them. The potatoes are then covered by the next cut of the plough, the field or plot finally being left level. . I have not seen many cases where the ground is drilled for potatoes as in Cheshire and Lancashire. I suppose the idea is that the ground will retain• the mqisture better, as it looks as if water is scarce in supamer.

``The local wells seem to be about 140 feet deep and as a windlass and rope is used, water is not thrown about anyhow. If anyone says: 'Just get me a bucket full of water' the person addressed has a slightly' harder task out here than at home. I am sure very few people in towns realise‘ how much they have to be thankful for in having a liberal supply of good water brought into their houses. At our last billet, after we had been there a week

===== 0002601_19160513_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTARY ORGANISATIONS.

Concert For Comforts For Our Soldiers.

The concert in Parr Hall on Saturday even- ing in aid of the Warrington District of the Voluntary Organisation Scheme approved by the Government for pro- viding comforts for soldiers, though an almost unqualified success from a musical point of view, was disappointing in not receiv- ing a more generous measure of public sup- port. With the services of the bandsmen of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment, under Mr. Cahill's conductorship, as a background to work upon— thanks to the kindness of Colonel Moggridge— the committee responsible for the concert ven- ture had secured a quite attractive array of musical helpers. Five vocalists, comprising Miss Marie Houghton, Madame Annie Har- greaves, Mr. Arthur W. Pearce, Mr. Wentworth Minty, and Captain J. Stott, and one instru- mentalist in the person of Miss Marie Xenaky, were concerned, along with the military band, in the performance of a programme which proved interesting as to the songs and pieces comprised in it, and highly praiseworthy as to the manner in which many of them were given. Among the four numbers set down for the band and several encore pieces, the most pleas- ing to listen to were the adaptation of Haydn's Farewell Symphony and the selection from Saint Saens's "Samson and Delilah," though these may not indicate the high-water mark attained in other items of the bandsmen's col- lective executant ability. The quaint humour found in the one, in the gradual withdrawal from the stage of the whole of the performers, and in the other, of the attainment of a fine climax at the rendering of the famous love- song, "Softly awakes my heart," made these pieces certain of securing the audience's ap- proval, the applause following their perform- ance being both vociferous and unstinted. In Miss Xenaky one quickly discerned a piano exponent whcise finished technique and artistic temperament were requisitioned with the hap- piest results in the B flat Scherzo of Chopin and in Liszt's No. 12 Rhapsodie. In both these pieces Miss Xenaky's delicate touch and well- restrained reserve of forcefulness were admir- ably displayed. The songs by Miss Marie Houghton in French. and Italian, while they served to show the flexibility and ample range of her well- trained voice, were not, possibly, wisely chosen for this particular occasion. Nevertheless, what she did was well received, and, being con- strained to respond to a recall, Miss Houghton gave "The Rosary" with all her accustomed artistic grace and expression. In the songs "All joy be thine" and "For you alone," Captain Stott evinced some difficulty in managing the voice, which was probably due to indisposition. Yet he was so far successful in pleasing the audience as to gain an encore. Some of the songs were, however, so excellently given as to call for special mention. First among these may be in- stanced "When you come home" and "My hameland," which made a powerful appeal to all who listened to them, not so much because of the, agreeable quality of Mde. Hargreaves' mezzo-soprano voice, as because of the depth of feeling and undisguised sincerity evidenced in her manner and style. Quite on the lines of a similar and highly-commendable self-abandon- ment was the singing of the Pagliacci prologue, the Toreador's song from "Carmen," and the quite different, but very beautiful song "I hear you calling me," by Mr. Arthur W. Pearce. A degree of artistic expression that was in many ways praiseworthy characterised several tenor songs given by Mr. Wentworth Minty, of which the best were " Teera's garden" and "Where my caravan has rested." Mr. E. Bennett North and Mr. W. H. Payton shared the honours of capably accompanying the various songs. In the interval Mr. J. H. Smethurst (chair- man of the local committee) thanked Colonel Moggridge for permission for the band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment to take part in the concert. The Colonel's kindness in connec- tion with the various concerts and charitable efforts in Warrington was well known. (Hear, hear.) Thanking the various artistes, Mr. Smethurst said the committee were also deeply grateful to Mr. Beddard for the energy he had displayed in arranging the concert. In regard to the object of the effort, he said the advantage of the Voluntary Organisation Scheme was that it was carried out under careful supervision in order to prevent overlapping. The local Terri- torial Equipment Fund, formed at the beginning of the war, and various other organisations were all working in harmony for the provision of comforts for the soldiers. The committee had been asked to provide a carriage for use in hos- pital wards. A great number of them were needed all over the country, and the committee honed, if their efforts that night were success- ful, to be able to instruct Sir Edward Ward to send fl. carriage to one of the local hospitals, suitably inscribed with the name of Warring- ton upon it (Apnlause )

===== 0002601_19160513_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR ON MAYERS.

Memorial Service To D.C.M. Who Was Killed.

The memory of the late Corporal Samuel Mayers, D.C.M., was honoured at Go'borne- street Baptist Church on Sunday evening. , The Pastor (the Rev. H. Ogden) conducted the service, and the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) also took part. The Mayor read the lessons, following which he addressed the con- gregation, which included relatives of the de- ceased.

His Worship said it only seemed a few weeks ago since he wrote a letter to the mother of the deceased soldier congratulating her because her son, by distinguished conduct, had earned the D.C.M. Now he slept with the brave men who had kept invasion from these, our shores. By his brave conduct he had added lustre to the fair name of the town, and shared with so many others the credit which they had brought to their birthplace. It was a perilous duty that he undertook when he won his D.C.M., and it was also a perilous avocation which he pursued in civil life. To sink mines was a work fraught with danger, and he (the Mayor) was proud that men who faced perils in time of peace for industrial prosperity, were more than will- ing to face even greater dangers for the safety and happiness of the Empire. To the mother of the lad who had gone, to his wife and chil- dren. and all members of the family, in the name of the town, he (the speaker) tendered his deep sympathy.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
A Tragic Event.

The Pastor, in the course of his sermon, said that to them it seemed most tragic that a life with yet a good 30 years' of service before it, should have been so suddenly cut off. For the fulfilment of God's purpose we must wait until "the day breaks and the shadows flee away." Corporal Mayers received his earliest religious instruction in that Church and Sundayi school. For seven or eight years he shared in the school and church life. He had been a member of tha choir and a regular and consistent member of the Bible Class. As the Mayor had said, in civil life he shared in common with so many working men an occupation of great peril. Mine- sinking demanded familiarity with death. At the outbreak of war he joined the Army as a volunteer, and almost from its commencement he had been in the strife. His heroism was a product of the soul. It was in deeds that men lived, not time, and he had added sacredness to human life by that heroism which gained its incentive from the soul. Mr. S. Bickers presided at the organ, and at the close of the service played the Dead March in "Saul," and " 0 Rest in the Lord" as a voluntary.

THEIR OLD SUNDAY SCHOOL.

Soldiers Send Their Quota To Anniversary Services.

. It is worthy of note that at the Silver-street Sunday School anniversary services, held in Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday, it was announced that several old scholars had sent various amounts to the collections, the con- tribution of one " Tommy " amounting to 10s. "We don't want the old school funds to suffer while we are away," was the burden of the letters of enclosure received.

A father contributed 10s. "as a thank offering that my son, amid the dangers of trench life. is still preserved to me and his country.'!

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

KILLED.

TWO BROTHERS-IN-LAW.

Double Bereavement For Patriotic Relatives.

More than usual sympathy will be felt with the relatives of a Warrington fireman and his brother-in-law, who have both met their deaths at the Front during the past fortnight. They are: Gunner William Pownall, a married man, of 33a, Golborne-street, and Private John Philip Ireland, a married man, of 77, Haydock- street. Writing to Mrs. Pownall, a Chaplain said that Gunner William Pownall, of the 62nd Trench Mortar Battery, R.G.A., had been admitted into hospital suffering from severe wounds, accident- ally received, and added that everything possible was being done for him. Superintendent Turner,

of the Fire Brigade, made further inquiries, and as a result he received a letter later in the week from the Chaplain, who said that Gunner Pow- nail "died bravely on the 22nd ult. He sent his love to his wife and children, and was buriel the following day." No news has yet been 'received as to how Gunner Pownalr received his wounds.

Gunner Pownall, who was 30 years of age, was a Reservist, having seen six years' service in India. He was born at Millwall, near London, but had lived at Warrington for quite a num- ber of years, being employed by the Warrington Corporation at Howley Yard, and later in the Patent House and .Wire Cleaning Department of Messrs. Rylands Bros.' works at Dalton Bank. He had been a member of the Warrington Fire Brigade for three years, and was greatly esteemed by officers and men, to whom news of his death comes as a great blow. Only a few clays before the news of his death was received, the Superintendent and Deputy-Superintendent each had a cheery letter from him, in which he said he was in the best of health. He leaves a widow and two 'children. A. brother of Mrs. Pownall, Private Joseph, of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment, has just returned to a military camp after having been at home re- covering from wounds. He has seen Ithirteen months' active service. Another brother, Thom-) c. is in hospital in Egypt suffering from dysentery.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
DIED FROM WOUNDS.

SERGT. E. HAMILTON.

Dies In Hospital From Severe

Wounds In The Chest.

News reached Mrs. Trevor, 28, Marsh-street, on Wednesday morning that her brother, Sergeant Edward Hamilton, of the Northumber- land Fusiliers, had died of wounds. The news was conveyed by Sister L. E. Jolley, who wrote under date May 7th, as follows:— "I am sorry to tell you that your brother, Sergeant Hamilton, was brought in here, No. 2 Casualty Clearing Station, very seriously wounded in the chest, and although everything possible was done for him, he passed peacefully away at 10.10 on Friday morning, May sth. He sent his love to you, and hoped you would not worry. He was buried in a part of the cemetery which is reseilred for our brave

troops, and a little cross, upon which is his name, &c., marks his resting place." Sergeant Hamilton, who was 33 years of age, had lived with his sister for five years. He enlisted in August, 1914, laving been previously employed at the Grappenhall Tannery, where he was exceedingly well liked by his workmates, for he was always of a jovial nature. He had been at the Front since June of last year, and was made sergeant on his 33rd birthday.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
AND MASTER FRANK SANDERS,

(hon. secretary), together with the members of the Finance Committee, have also very kindly offered to carry on the work of the association during his absence, and all communications should be addressed to Councillor Wilkinson as chairman. Ever since the commencement of the war Mr. Sanders, by permission of the association, has, along with Mr. Hayes, the Conservative Agent, strenuously and successfully striven to promote voluntary recruiting in every way possible, and to further other equally necessary work asso- ciated with the conduct of the war.

Recruiting campaigns, Red Cross, Russian, Serbian, Belgian and French flag days, soldiers' and sailors' employment scheme, Derby scheme organisations, the attestation of recruits, the carrying out of the military register; and many other similar projects, found full employment for the agents of both sides since the fateful day in August, 1914, when war was declared. The work so carried out, and freely given, has proved to be of great value to the nation, and has been acknowledged by the Prime Minister, Lord Kitchener, Lord Derby and public author- ities.

Mr. Sanders has many well-wishers, but the action of the Women's Liberal Association, who gave him a good send-off on Monday and pre- sented him with the usual soldier's outfit, has given him•the greatest pleasure. Mrs. Osborne, after a brief but kindly farewell speech, called for three cheers, which were heartily raised. Mr. Sanders very feelingly responded, Our photograph shows Mr. Sander, accom- panied by his wife, Mrs. Sanders, the well- known contralto singer, and their little son, Frank.

or so and the people were getting nice and friendly, they told us that a Frenchman had committed suicide about two years ago by drop- ping down their well, and he Was still there as far as they. knew. As they were using the well and seemed healthy enough, we did not discontinue using ,it, but took care to use, chlorinated water for drinking purposes from another source, which had been analysed. War is full of surprises, is it not?

"Please pardon this long screed, and again with many thanks, I am, sincerely yours, BRERETON FAIRCLOUGH."

VOLUNTEER ORDERS.

SOUTH-WEST LANCASHIRE REGT.

Warrington Company.

BRIDEGROOM KILLED.

THE LATE PRIVATE FLETCH

A war wedding at Golborne-street Baptist Church in November has had a sad sequel in the death in action of the bridegroom'. He was Private Percy Fletcher, of the South Wales Borderers, and lived at St. Barnabas'-place, Lovely-lane. ,His widow was Miss Agnes Free- man, daughter of the late Mr. John Freeman, of Walker-street.

Mk•.

ONE OF MANY HEROES.

Private John Philip Ireland, of the West Riding Regiment, was killed in action on ths 26th ult. Official news has ben received to that effect, while the following letter has also reache I

PRIVATE JOHN P. IRELAND.

Killed.

Mrs. Ireland from Captain Malcolm Robertson: "I just want to add a few words of sympathy from his old company commander on the death of your husband. Ile was a fine, well-trained, soldier, and was always of the greatest use in the company. He was last seen alive helping a stretcher-bearer (who was also killed) to bring in wounded during a terrible bombardment. The company 'did splendidly, and every man was a hero who stuck it, for they drove the Germans back. It will be a terrible blow to you and your family, but you may still be proud of a good husband for the way he did his duty."

Private Ireland was 39 years of age, and leaves a widow and seven children. He also was an old soldier, having served in the Boer War. Ha was not a Reservist, however, but enlisted of his own accord in September, 1914. He was for- merly employed by Mr. J. Starkey, metal dealer, Mersey-street, as a carter. It is only five weeks ago since he returned to the Front, after a well- earned furlough.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE E. MORRIS. One Who Served In Three Theatres Of War.

News has reached Miss Morris, at 37, Hop- wood-street, that her brother, Private Edwin Morris, of the South Lancashire Regiment, has been killed in action.

Private Morris, who had been in the Army for seven months, saw service at the Dardan- elles, Egypt and the Persian Gulf. While in Egypt he had a bad attach of jaundice and was in hospital for a considerable time. He was 26 years of age, and before enlisting was employed by-Mr. Richardson, painter and paper hanger, Egypt-street. He was educated at the National School, Church-street.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
SERGEANT T. REGAN.

ReCOWS Bayonet AVound In The Left Thigh.

Mrs. Regan, 95, West-street, has received news that her husband, Sergeant Thomas Regan, of the 6th South Lancashire Regiment, has re- ceived a bayonet wound in the left thigh, but, according to a letter from the Sergeant himself, he is progressing favourably. Sergeant Regan is 32 years of age, and before enlisting, in the first month of the war, was employed as a carter by Mr. H. Gandy, corn dealer. Be was a member of the Wycliffe P.S.A.

DIED FROM GAS POISONING _ PRIVATE ,C. SHEPLEY.

Dies From Gas Poisoning After Five Weeks' Service At Front.

Although only 18 years of age, Private Charles Shepley, of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, and son of Mr. and Mrs. Shepley, 18, Dutton-street, Howley, has laid down his life for his country. News to that effect has reached his mother in the form of the following letter, dated May sth, from D. C. Williams, an officer of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers:—

"It is my painful duty to inform you that on Sunday morning last the opemy made a gas attack 6'n 4ur lines, and your son was affected by it and died. He could not have suffered for any length of time. I officiated at the burial, when his body and those of four others were laid to rest. Your son died doing his duty. As he

had so lately come to us. I cannot personally speak much of him, but I would wish you to accept my sincere sympathy. Everything pos- sible was done for him. You are sure of the blessing of submission, and however we may be able to explain, we should not like to think other than the Lord is in some way behind all, and He doeth all things well, and can over- rule all circumstances."

Private Shepley had only been at the Front five weeks when he met his death, he having enlisted seven months ago. He was formerly employed by Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, and previously by .the Whitecross Company. He was educated at the National School, and wor- shipped at St. Clement's. Mission. He also be- longed to the Church Lads' Brigade there. His father is a stonemason employed by the War- rington Corporation.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE ARTHUR PAYNE.

Death & Funeral Of Respected Sankey Soldier.

It is with regret that we learn of, the death of another of our brave lads in the person of Private Arthur Payne, the news of which came as a terrible shock to his parents at 25, Hood- lane, Sankey, on Thursday morning of last week. Two telegrams arrived simultaneously from the London General Hospital, although both were sent off at different times the pre- vious evening, the one to say that the Private was seriously ill and the other stating he had passed away. Private Payne was 30 years of age, and prior to enlistment was employed by the Mersey White Lead Company. He was a member of the "Tom Sangster" Lodge of the and was held in the highest esteem by all who knew him. He enlisted in the South Lancashire Regiment in August, 1914, and went on active service in February, 1915. Last June he took part in the battle of Hooge, and sustained internal injuries through being crushed when the parapet of the trench was blown up. After serving con- tinuously for twelvs months, he got home on furlough in February last. For some consider- able time he had been troubled with bronchitis. and during tha tan days' furlough he seemed to make very little improvement. However, he , went back to the trenches again, and was in-

valided to London front the Front just over three weeks ago.

Funeral Attended By Crowds.

The funeral took place on Sunday at Sankey C2lnetb.;•y, tile chief mourners at the graveside being: Mr. M Payne (father), Messrs. -Miry, Fred and Walter (brothers), T. Phillips (brother-in-law), G. Ifeeion (uncle), W. A. Bromley and J. Bond (friends). The body was borne by Messrs. R. Nelson, W. Cook, W. Nor- bury, P. Phillips, A. Jones and' G. Roberts, all intimate friends of the deceased. Prior to the departure for the Cemetery a service was held in Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Chapel, which place of worship the family attend, and although Private Payne had not been connected with the place fqr some years, he had passed through the Sunday school, and then was at- tracted by the Brotherhood at Wycliffe, of which he remained a member till the last.

The Rev. John Gunnell preached at the memorial service, and the chapel was filled- to overflowing. The choir was in attendance, and the hymns selected for the service were " Jesu, Lover of My Soul " and "My God, My Father, While I Stray." As the body was borne from the chapel Mr. Levi Richardson, who officiated at the organ, played .the Dead March in "Saul." As the cortege passed along the route was thronged with sympathisers, and on arrival at the Cemetery many hundreds were observed to have assembled

there. At the graveside the Rev. John Gunnell conducted the burial service, after which Bro. John Wolfenden, R.0.M., K.G.11., acting chap- lain for the Buffaloes, conducted a most im- pressive and touching service. The R.A.0.8. were, in addition, represented by Bro. G. Davies, R.0.H., P.P.G.P.; Bro. W. Chadwick, C.P.; Bro. Craig, C.P.; Bro. Steward, K.0.M.; Bro. Kennewell, C.P., K.G.H.; Bro. Kirkham, C.P., a Sergeant in R.A.M.C. khaki and W.P. of the "Tom Sangster" Lodge, along with his officers. The "Queen Alexander" Lodge was also well represented, along with the combined lodges of the Warrington District. The Buffaloes' Band would have been in attendance, but the rela- tives wished it otherwise. Some of the de- ceased's comrades in arms who were home from the Front were present.

MARRIED MEN'S RELIEF.

Outline Of Government Scheme To Assist With Liabilities.

With the Service Bill well on its way to the Statute Book and the calling out of the last Derby groups, it becomes urgently necessary, writes the Parliamentary Correspondent of the "Times," that the Government scheme for assisting men to meet their civil liabilities should be put into operation without delay. The necessary regulations have been framed by the Local Government Board and passed on to the Treasury for their sanction. It is expected that the regulations, as finally approved, will be made public towards the end of next week. They will then be available at every Post Office in Great Britaiii, together with a supply of forms to be filled up by applicants for grants. These forms will be rather .formidable docu- ments, requiring a great deal of information in detail of the domestic position of the applicant. Grants In Aid.

The regulations will make no material change in the scheme outlined by the Prime Minister during the secret session. Accordingly any man: single or married, who has joined the forces since August 4th, 1914, will be able to apply for grants to help him to meet such liabilities as rent, mortgage interest, payments in instal- ments on buildings and furniture, taxes, rates, insurance premiums, and school fees. The assistance to be granted will in no case exceed £lO4 a year. The cases will be invested locally by barristers. About 60 barristers will be appointed as a beginning, and :more will be added if it is found that the work is too much for them. Each county area will have its own barrister-Commissioner. Certain populous areas will be sub-divided. London, for instance, will have six or seven Commissioners, each hearing claims from a different district. The whole machinery will have been set in motion by the end of the nionth. Each appli- cant will have to fill up a form and send it to his local Cqmmissioner. The proceedings before the Commissioner will be strictly private and confidential. To meet the cases of Men dt the front or in training-camps at hothe, wives or other persons armed with the necessary authority will be allowed to make claims in per- son before the Commissioners. After this per- sonal investigation the Commissioners will make recommendations to Mr. Hayes Fisher's Central Committee in London, who will proceed to make the necessary grants. It has not yet been finally decided whether there shall be an appeal from the recommenda- tions of the Commissioners. There is little doubt however that if an applicant finds that he has fresh facts to lay before the authorities be will be allowed to do so. The Local Govern- ment Board and the Treasury are still quite unable to make any estimate of the number of applications which will be made and of the sum which the grants will absorb. That the first will run into hundreds of thousands and the second into millions sterling is fully expected.
