LiYMM

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC.

I see that the 26th and 27th of this month are to be observed as "flag days" in our district. " Anothex flag to the old collection."

Ylutbful maidens with bewitching smiles Who can help patronising them?

The latest in songs: "Won't you buy my pretty flags?"

A shooting match took place at the Drill Hall on Tuesday night, Lymm v. Stockton Heath.

Owing to indisposition two of the Stockton Heath team were unable to attend, and were deputised by two of the Lymm Platoon, who managed to rake them in a victory by one point.

I hear that these two gentlemen are likely to go "through it" next drill night.

The latest charity craze! Jumble sale and. entertainment combined, with refreshments thrown in. What a grand time we are going to have!

Nevertheless, we are asked to remember the. cause. Cheshire soldiers' shirt and sock fund.

"Tommy "has been giving the Huns "socks," so in return we are asked to _contribute towards " Tommy's " socks.

A gentleman asked me the other day if I knew, or had any idea where our overflow services would be held when the suggested prize scheme came into operation. I don't; do you?

I am very pleased to hear that Sergeant T. W. Field (our local D.C.M.) is well on the road to recovery. •

"The nation and the war" was the subject taken by Mr. Frank Milne, a well-known Liberal orator, who preached at the Congregational Church last Sunday.

I see that some of our local soldiers organised a whist drive among themselves somewhere in France.

The first prize was a watch, and was won by Private James Gilberts, a Newfields soldier. "How's the time, Jimmy?"

A few weeks ago I gave a hint to would-be charity organisers that we wanted a whist drive. Now, I believe, we are going to have one on Wed- nesday at the garden fete. "Welcome little stranger."

A friend of mine has this week written to tell me about .the girls who are employed in his office. but being a descendent of Burns, the Scotch poet, he decided to tell me in verse, and this is how it goes:—

Girls At The Office. The office is really a different place, And every man works with a smile on his face, It's "After you, Miss!" as we bow with a grace, Because we have girls at the office.

The office boy formerly looked such a wreck. But now with clean collars his form he'll bedeck, Indeed, it is whispered he washes his neck, Because we have girls at the office.

We " Mister " each other Most formally now, And never by any chance kick up a row, Our conduct is excellent, all must allow, Because we have girls at the office.

From using strong language the seniors all shirk, And the manager never goes out for a drink, The effort it costs them you really can't think, Because we have girls at the office.

Where it will end no one knows—no one cares, But it makes one careful about what one wears, And it's costing now about double in fares, For those who work at the office. J. W. E.

Military Tribunal.—A firm of salt and chemi- cal manufacturers appealed to the local Tri- bunal for the release from military service of nine of their workmen. The case was before the Tribunal on April 6th, when it was stated that the firm were expecting the men to be badged. The case was postponed and a repre- sentative of the firm now asked the Tribunal to grant a further adjournment, as the author- ities had not yet granted the badges. On an undertaking being given that the Court would be communicated with as soon as the point in question had been settled, an adjournment was agreed to.

Driver George Leigh Receives His Discharge.— Driver George Leigh, of the A.S.C., has this week received his discharge from the military authorities. He enlisted at the outbreak, and was drafted out to the East, where he contracted dysentery. He returned to England and has since been an inmate of the Belton Park Miii- tary Hospital, Grantham. He is a steelworker, and is employed by Wm. Robertson, of Grappen- hall. He resides with his widowed mother at West Hyde, Newfields. He has two brothers serving with the colours.

Blue Cross Fund: A Leiter Of Thanks.—Miss Evelyn Wright, of Avondale, Cherry-lane, one oi the organisers of the Blue Cross concert, has received the following letter from the treasurer of the Blue Cross fund :—" Dear Madam,— Thank you for your letter and cheque value '£B 168. 5d., being the proceeds of your concert in ,aid of the Blue Cross fund, for which I enclose official receipt. Please allow me to offer you on behalf of the President, Lady Smith-Dorrien, and the committee their very sincere thanks for•this valuable help towards our work.—l am, your truly (signed) Arthur F. Coke, hon. treas."

Home From The Front.—Private Richara Shaw, of the Cheshire Regiment, has this week arrived home from the Front. Talking to our representative, he informed him that he left the Lymm boys in the best of health, and still on the "pioneer job." They are at present a few miles behind the firing line, but were expecting to be moved up any time., The boys are very fond of the work -they are doing, because they are always sure of a good night's rest, and get their meals pretty regularly. "We always," he says, "make our spare time as happy as pos- sible, and organise between ourselves various games and competitions. The last one we had was a whist drive, when a watch was offered as first prize, and was won by James Gilberts, a Newfields soldier." Private Shaw was mobolised as a member of the Cheshire Regiment at the outbreak of war, and went out in February of last year, but came back again to England early in the summer suffering from a wound in the leg. He was eventually drafted out again early last autumn. He is a itondon and North- Western Railway Company employe, and resides at Booth's Hill.

Sapper Joseph Clare Returns to the Front.— Mrs. J. Clare, Booth's Hill, Lymm, has this week received a letter from her son, Sapper Joseph Clare, of the Royal Engineers, notifying her to the effect that he has landed in France once again. The letter reads as follows :—" At last I have landed once again on French soil, and Dick Shaw, one of my old comrades, has just left here for Blighty.' Herbert Rigby is also expecting to come any day. Harold's (his brother) time is getting very short now, and he will soon have to come back, but anyway, I hope that he has had a good time. Now don't upset yourself if you don't hear from me again this week, as we have had strict orders not to write more than two letters a week, and I dare say that the same order will apply to ,us when we reach the battalion. Keep on smiling and remember me to all at Lymm." Sapper Joseph Clare, it will be remembered, was wounded some time ago in the mouth, and came over, to England, where he was nursed back to health at the Whitworth-street Military Hos- pital, Manchester. ", He is employed by the London and North-Western Railway Company r'a lehourer. His brother, Sergeant-Major H. Clare, is at present on furlough from the Front.

RECTOR COMMENDS ECONOMY.

Wants Every One With Under £3OO A Year To Join Savings 'Club.

"In connection with our duty- to our country at this time comes also with insistent voice the paramount duty of economy and saving for the expenses of the war," declares the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston) in his monthly letter in the "Parish Magazine." "1 could not if I would write to you a treatise on political economy, but I can set down a few plain faots that should be under- stood by all. A country has entered upon the high road to ruin when the goods it purchases from abroad cannot be paid for by the goods manufactured at home and sold abroad; tbe balance, it is obvious, must be paid in gold, the life blood of a nation's financial existence. This is what is happening to us and to other nations at the present time. The manhood of the countries is occupied in destructive warfare, and the resources of the countries are being eaten up by the munitions of war, and by the cost of keeping such immense armies in the field. It is obvious, therefore, that ordinary pro- ductive commerce is practically at a standstill, and hence comes the need of saving all we can. This is what is meant when we are told that this war will be won by ' silver ' bullets, in other words by those nations who can save the most. There are being set up all over England War Savings Clubs, to which the poorest can con- tribute; you will find particulars of these associations in a paper that will be •distributed with the "Magazine." The certified office in Lymrn is the Boys' School, and money can be deposited there every Saturday evening between the hours of 6.30 and 7.30. Already over thirty certificates have been bought in three weeks, and it is honed that every one with an income of under £3OO a year will join: sums from sixpence upwards can be deposited."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
A LOSS TO LYIIII.

Death Of .111 r. A. B. Botimphrey.

We regret to announce the death of Mr. A. B. Boumphrey, which took place at his residence, Baycliffe, on Thursday morning, after a long illness, at the age of 59. Deceased was widely known and highly respected in the district, and his death will mean a serious loss to Lymm. Being a large cotton merchant he became widely known throughout Lancashire. He was a member of both the Manchester and Liver- pool Cotton Exchanges, and some years ago he held the office of president to the Manchester Chamber of Commerce. Deceased leaves a widow, five sons and two daughters to mourn their loss. Four of the sons hold commissions in His Majesty's Forces, and are at present on active service.

The interment will take place to-day at 3.15 p.m. at the Parish Church.

WIDOWS PATRIOTIC RECORD.

Three Sons With The Colours And One On Government Work.

Lymm possesses some fine patriotic families, and the latest to come to our notice is that of Mks. Sarah Ann Daniels (a widow), of The Rookery, Booth's Hill, who claims to have three sons and three nephews at present serving with the Colours, a son-in-law has been killed in action, and the only remaining son at home is engaged on Government work. Interviewed by our representative yesterday, Mrs. Daniels informed him that-her eldest son, Harry Daniels, Wakefield, was an old soldier, and served in the Loyal North Lancs. Regiment during the Boer War. For this service he is the proud possessor of two medals (King's and Queen's). At the outbreak of war he again volunteered for service in his old regiment, and is at present stationed in the South of England. In civil life- he follows the occupation of a labourer, and prior to the war was engaged in the making of the new Lever road. He is a married man, and resides with his wife and child at the Rookery, Booth's Hill. Another son is Private James Daniels, also a married man, who resides at The Rookery, Booth-% Hill. He enlisted at the outbreak of war, and was drafted out to France in July of last year, where he has remained ever since. In civil life he is a tanner, employed by the Grap-• penhall Tanning Co. - • The other son is Private Joseph Daniels, also a married man, residing at The Rookery, Booth's Hill. He enlisted at the ,outbreak of war, and joined the South Lancs. Regiment. Early in June of last year he was drafted out to the Dardanelles, and re- turned wounded in September, but has since

A WORD ABOUT THE CAMERON HIGHLANDERS.

By PRIVATE ERNEST SHAW. (Continued.)

"January, 1915.—What a remarkable New Year! Little did we think when we wished cue another the old wish, how many of us would he living to see the dawn of another year. You wonder whether it really could be a happy New Year, but it was. • When we woke on New Year's morning we were as happy a crowd as one could wish to meet.. Little did the 'dogs of war trouble us, and although we had an occasional 'Jack Johnson' that our friends, the Huns sent us with their compliments, we did not trouble in the least, but hurriedly returned the compliment with interest. Last w-.ek. I told you about the first Christmas of the war, and if you take yourself back to the 28th of Decem- ber you will find that we were billeted in a tobacco factory. Well, at this plae,3 a m.ist km- usual incident,occurred. The factory was two storeys high, and troops were billeted in both departments. Soon after arriving we got the order that there would, be a rifle inspection in an hour's time, so we at once set to work and cleaned our rifles. Now it happened that cue man in the bottom department, who had just come off guard duty, had failed to unload his rifle, and when he was rubbing the mud off the metal work, he caught the trigger, with the result that the bullet passed clean through the roof above and entered the foot of a comrade who was cleaning his rifle in' the top department. It was a regrettable incident, but, nevertheless, it was a lesson to all of us.

"But-let us get back to January, things'did not seem to go well with us; we had plenty of casualties, plenty of bad weather, and what seemed to us poor results, but really it was just the referse, and as you will see by the diary below, our life was anything but a bed of roses. "January Ist (New Year's Day).—Marched af 7.30 a.m. to , where we were crowded into small billets.

"January 2nd.—Moved at 6.30 p.m. and took over a line of trenches from the Sussex Regi- ment. Weather very muddy and' wet. "January 11th.—Lieutenant Henderson sent down to the base, suffering with a high tem- perature due to exposure. Hardships of the life telling heavily on the rank and file. "January 12th.—At 6 p.m. Ist Battalion Gloucesters arrived to relieve us. Companies marched out independently, the last one arriving at 2.30 a.m. on the 13th, and went into billets (51 miles). A draft of 100 men joined the Bat- talion, under Captain Bethune.

"January 13th.—Marched back to tobacco factory. Men in a filthy state, covered with mud.

"January 14th.--Spent the day cleaning up

"January 15th.—Second-Lieutenant Gordon rejoined. Five sets of pipes recovered from base; four sets taken into use.

"January 18th.—Managed to get .a hot bath. "January 17th.—Major-Generitl.Walked round billets, and spoke to men individually. "January 18th.—A great downfall of snow, "January 19th.—A draft of 138 arrived from

"January 21st.—After inspection by Major- General, marched 54 miles to village, and re- lieved the Loyal North Laneashires in trenches. Trenches very wet and middy, but not so law] as those we had previously occupied. "January 23rd.—Relieved by Ist Scots Guards; marched into billets 24 miles away. "January 24th.—Resting. • "January 25th.—Marched back under sudden orders (2 miles) to support the Scots Guards, who had been attacked. "January 26th.—Lieut. Methuen wounded. A draft of 50 arrived from. . Loyal North Lancs. relieved us after dark, and we marched to our billets (5 miles). "January 27th.—Second-Lieutenants McDer- mot and Kinmont sent down to the base with 'swollen feet.

"January 28th.—Hard frost. 5.45 p.m., sudden march (3 miles), occupied old billets. "January 29th.-9 a.m., moved to village and stayed in shelter of houses all day. At night relieved L.N. Lancs. in trenches. "January 30th.—Relieved by Irish Guards about 7 p.m. Marched six miles and got into billets about 10.30 p.m. "January 31st.—Marched to billets in village 10 miles away. Snowed heavily all day. "And that finished the month of January, and left a few incidents in my mind which I shall never forget."

STOCKTON HEATH.

PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

The Waste Paper Nuisance And Street Lighting.

There was little business of public interest transacted at the annual meeting of the Stock- ton Heath Parochial Committee, held in the Council Schools on Tuesday night. There were present Messrs. J. H. Clarke, F. Pearson, J.

Hallows, C. W. Hallows, F. Warburton, J. McDermid, W. Mclntyre, W. E. Hough (clerk) and S. Wright (surveyor). Mr. E. Bradshaw (Clerk of the Parish Council) was also present. In Mr. G. Pilling's absence, the chair was taken by Mr. Clarke. Mr. J. Hallows moved that the retiring Chair- man (Mr. G. Pilling), the Vice-Chairman (Mr. J. 11. Clarke) and the various committees be reappointed, and this was agreed. The Clerk read a letter from Mr. Spensley, nightsoil contractor, offering to remove and destroy waste paper from the ashbins for ss. per week. Mr. Mclntyre expressed the opinion that it would be a good thing if the committee could get the public into the habit of destroying all waste paper.—The over was accepted. A letter was read from the Gas Committee of the Warrington Corporation, in reply to a letter from that committee, stating that their charge of ss. per lamp for lighting the district was the minimum they could accept.

KNUTSFORD DIVISION.

Annual Meeting Of The conservative Association.

Colonel Dixon presided over the annual meet- ing of the Knutsford Division Conservative and Unionist Association, held last Saturday at the Knutsford Conservative Club. Lord Newton was re-elected as president, and the vice-presi- dents were also re-elected. Other ,re-elections were :—Chairman, Colonel Dixon; Vice-Chair-

man, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart.; Hon. Treasurer, Captain Egerton Leigh. To represent the Association at the Cheshire Division of the National Union the following were appointed : Colonel Dixon and Messrs,. J. A. Wanklyn, S. W. Gillet, A. Hodgkinsan and G.. Duckworth. Colonel Sykes And Our Share In The War.

Colonel A. J. Sykes, M.P., in seconding the adoption of the annual report, which had been submitted from the chair, referred to the many failures made by Germany, and said that we had no reason to be despondent as to our pos1;. tion to-day. The Government had been run down for its want of preparations-, but we were never a military nation before the war. Before the war we numbered the Army in thousands, but to-day we numbered it in millions. That surely was something of an achievement, and when people said we should have had conscrip- tion months ago, we had to consider that be- sides getting the men—he admitted the import- ance of getting the men—there was the question of arming and equipping them, and the men were not wanted before the time we could actually use them. There were other things be- sides providing the men. We were under an obligation to the Allies to supply them not only with men in the figaing Tine but with other things just as vitally necessary. The Allies looked to England for money, and England had to look ahead to see where the necessary amount was to come from, for the war was going to be a long one. It might not be finished this year, or even next year. We could only keep up the supplies to the Allies by keeping up our export trade. Russia was not able to carry on her offensive because of lack of supplies. France had lost her manufacturing centre, and there- fore we had, to supply her with all the necessi- ties for carrying on the war. We might trust safely the present chief of staff at the War Office. The position to-day was infinitely better for the Allies than was the ease a year ago. We appreciated more fuffy the immensity of the task undertaken, but we were more determined than a year ago not to take our hand from the plough until we eaped the right and proper harvest for the Toss we had sustained, and the harvest we would reap was the imposition on Prussian brutality of such a peace that the hundred-headed hydra would not raise one single head for the next hundred years.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
PAD GATE.

Come Through Smiling.—An interesting letter has been received by Mr. Percy Royston, of Paddington, from-Mr. A. E. Faulks, of Padgate- lane, who is on active service in France. He says: "'I was quite interested in your brother's chat with the Scotch V.C., and I know it would stimulate his efforts in this great cause. Sorry to hear about General Townsend having to sur- render, but we mustn't grumble, as I think the majority of people didn't expect him to hold out this long. There is only one bad point about it, and that is, it will be a bit more for the croakers to shout about. We are having some very fine weather just at present, so they have extended the tours of duty in the trenches to eight days. The last eight we were in there was considerable straffing, going on by the artil- lery on both sides, but I can assure you ours came out on the top. Of course, it is a bit warm for the boys in the trenches while it is on, but I am pleased to say they mostly come through the ordeal smiling. I did not mention in my last letter about the battalion winning the Divisional Football Cup and medals, and the majority of the regiment had the pleasure of drinking out of it. We beat the R.A.M.C. 1-0, so you can guess it was a fairly good gan,e." Speaking of the Army, .he says it is not at all a bad life, providing a man is prepared to rough it and knuckle under .a little.

PENKETH.

THE BARN FIRE.

Prisoner Discharged At Liverpool

Assizes.

A charge of arson-a-i-Tenketh was preferred against Samuel Harold Kemp (22), farm labourer, at the Liverpool Assizes on Saturday, before Mr. Justice Low.

Mr. Riley, prosecuting, said that in the evening of April 11th a Dutch barn, filled with hay was fired. Two young men named Phcenix and Fisher saw the prisoner coming from the side of the barn, and almost at the same moment smoke was seen. Matches were found upon him, and he re- marked to the police-constable who arrested him: "I expect this will be a two or three years' job for me, but the more the merrier." Corroborative evidence was given of counsel's statement: The damage done amounted to £3OO. Mr. Madden appeared for the prisoner, who pleaded not guilty, and stated that he went on the farm grounds for a purpose of his own. He lit a cigarette and stamped,on the match. He was forty yards away from the haystack, and helped Phcenix and Fisher to save some lorries from the fire The jury ietuited a verdict of "Not guilty:',

Scholars Entertain Wounded Soldiers.—A party of wounded soldiers were entertained 'o tea on Saturday afternoon by the teachers and scholars of the Croft Council School. NOT MUCH. ENCOURAGEMENT.

Government Regulations Retard The Keeping Of Lodgers. An ineffective protest against the regulations of the Defence of the Realm Act was made by George Lightfoot, wheelwrig,ht, of Whitley Superior, at the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, when he was summoned for keeping a lodging-house without registering particulars about the lodger. NORWEGIAN SERVANT GIRL.

Fined For Failing To Register Herselk

As Allen.

Living at Whitley Superior is a Norwegian servant girl named Inga Johnson, who, although a friend of England, was fined 10s. at the Dares- bury Petty Sessions; held at Stockton Heath on Tuesday, for not having registered, and also for having transferred herself from one prohibited area, that of Northwich, to another prohibited area, that of Runcorm Rural. District, without the permission of the Registration Officer. It was explained by Mr. H. J. Westbrook, who appeared for the defence, that Miss Johnson had been in England since 1909, and had been in service in various parts of he country. When. the war broke out she was engaged in that way with a Miss Smith, of 'Hale, Cheshire. She left her and went to Hartford, where she was employed by Mr. Brock, managing director for Messrs. Brunner, Mond and Co. It was a serious thing that no one seemed to have warned' her as to what was required by our Government regulations, and it had been left to Mr. Fair-- hurst, who had engaged her at Whitley Superior, to put her in the way of righting her- self with the authorities. She only came to Mr. Fairhurst'S house on' the 13th April. and bore a most excellent character. Within two hours of being told that she ought to obtain an aliens' identity book from Runcorn, she was in a motor car on her way to that place for that purpose. She had acted entirely in ignorance. It was difficult for an Englishman to follow the- regulations, and he could well understand a lack of comprehension on the part of a girl in her position, especially as she was living mostly in country places. He added that she was a friend of England.

LOCAL HELPERS.

If our readers need encouragement and help, surely both will be found in such an earnest message as the one we are privileged to give to-day.

It is from a local man, and rings with sincerity. Read it and bear in mind that it is only one of many such stirring messages. On March 16th, 1915, ,Mr. T. Smith. of 33, Melville-street, Warrington, said :—" For fully three months I was so crippled with lumbago that I had to walk about with my back bent like an old man. The pain used to catch me right at the bottom of my back and strike down my loins. It was quite like a knife going through me, and I became so bad that I hardly dared move. How I managed to keep about, I don't know. I was in pain all the time, day and night, and continual suffering almost wore me out. I tried plasters and embrocations, and I got a belt which cost me.five guineas, but I am sorry to say I got no relief. I began to think I should never get well again. "It was through reading a testimonial that I tried Doan's Backache Kidney Pills. I had nit taken many doses of these pills before I found that they were relieving me. Needless to say, I perseverOd with them, and it was not long before I was quite cured. Doan's Pills have been worth their weight in gold to me, and I am sure they would be to anyone who suffers with a bad back." (Signed) "T. Smith." On February 17th, 1916—eleven months later— Mr. Smith said :—" I have never had any serious return of lumbago since Doan's Pills made such a fine cure of me. I feel I can't say too much in their praise." Lumbago is rheumatism of the muscles of the back and loins. You wake up in the mornings with a crippling pain in the back,- and sharp pains dart through the loins when stooping or rising from a chair. Lumbago is caused by uric • acid irritating and inflaming the muscles or nerves of the loins. Doan's Backache Kidney Pills herp the kidneys to throw off the poisonous acid through the urinary system. Of all dealers, or 2/9 a ,box, from Foster' McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, Oxford-street, London, W. Don't ask for backache or kidney' pills—ask distinctly for Doon's Backaohe Kidney Pills, the same as Mr. Smith had.

REDCAP!

Alleged Deserter Who Posed As Military Policeman.

CORRECTED OFFICERS IN PUBLIC.

Having paraded the streets of Warrington for a fortnight or more as a member of the military Police, Charles Edward Hooper, of the was in the surprising position of being handed over as a military deserter by the borough magis- trates on Monday. He is a young, dark end smart-looking fellow, and by reason of wearing cap with a red crown, was known as " Redcap:- among the soldiers and police in the town. Inspector Hodgkiss said the prisoner was standing at Market Gate on Saturday, and was questioned by Detective-Sergeant Reddington,as to who he. was. He replied that he was a sergeant-major of military police, having been sent by the Provost Marshal at Chester to supervise the conduct of soldiers in Warrington and district. It had been ascertained that Hooper was entirely unknown at Chester, and he had sing') admitted that he was an absentee from the K.S.L.I. He had been pulling up Army men —not only those of the rank and file, but officers —and correcting them regarding their dress. Mr. S. W. Wright (a magistrate) : I have seen him doing it myself. The Chief Constable then added that Hooper had been staying in lodgings and had fleeced some unfortunate people to the extent of £3. They had even bought him a new pair of boots. All those facts would be mentioned to the mili- tary authorities. .Hooper was then handed over.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
"AN EXTRAORDINARY

GIRL."

Thought Post Office Savings

Bank Unsafe In War-Time,

Contending that the prisoner—a tea hawker of 19 years—had an extraordinary temperament, being liable to forget important matters and fail to appraise them correctly, Mr. T. S. Steel, at the Borough Police Court on Monday, persuaded the magistrates to dismiss Stella Davies, of 23, Ashton-street, Liverpool, on a charge of stealing is. 3d., the money of Mrs. Wilkinson, of 29, Amelia-street, Warrington. It was stated by Mrs. Wilkinson, who is the wife of a soldier, that on Tuesday of last week Miss Davies called at her house and endeavoured to persuade her to buy some packets of Empire Bonus Tea. At first she declined, saying she did not drink tea, but the girl was persistent and wedged her foot so- that the door could not be closed. One of her remarks was, "Well, even if you don't drink the tea straight away, it will last you for years." Witness consented to take two packets of tea, and on giving her a half- crown—is. 3d. more than the price of the tea— Davies ran off and witness was unable to catch her. Two days afterwards Mrs. Wilkinson saw Davies near the Central Station, and gave information to P.C. Macdonald, who arrested her. Davies then denied that "she had ever been in Warrington before, and declared emphatically. that she had never seen Mrs. Wilkinson or known anything about the half-crown.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
Calls As Memory Restorer.

Mr. Steel, who defended, said he was told that his client was an extraordinary individual. She was hysterical and did most unusual things at home. When spoken to• she seemed to realise what was said, but very shortly afterwards for- got all about it. Then, later, it all came back to her. That was what had happened in this case. She received half a crown for the tea and ran off to several shops to get change and being in a district where there were many streets, none of which. she knew, she forgot the address of Mrs. Wilkinson. She did not intend to steal the money, and the only way in which be (Mr. Steel) could account- for that was that she had com- pletely forgotten the incident for the time being. "When did you remember about the half- crown?" asked the Chief Constable, when the prisoner entered the witness box. "It all came back to me in the cells," she replied. Mr. Steel mentioned that soon after the war broke out the prisoner withdrew £2 from the Post Office Savings Bank because she thought it was not safe there. She had carried the money about with her ever since. After the girl had given evidence, Dr. Joseph remarked that he had noticed that after most of the auestions had been put to her she stood dazed, as if not quite understanding them—as if trying to recollect. The Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) announced that the magistrates had considered the case very carefully, and whilst there were sus- picious elements about it, they had decided by a majority to dismiss the charge.

Conscientious Objector's Oratory Cut

Short.

BRUTAL ASSAULT.

Irishman Told To Expend His Violence On The Germans.

A story of an Irishman's brutal and nnpro- I's:wilted assault upon a Warrington Corporation employe was related to the borough magistrates on Wednesday, when Patrick Dunbar, labourer, of no fixed abode, was charged with being drunk and disorderly and' assaulting John Ross, aged 77.

He Proceeded there with Special Constable Dendy, and found prisoner being detained by Mr. Stirrup and Mr. Brooks, two local licensees. John Ross stated that on Tuesday afternoon he was sweeping Fennel-street, when without a vrord of warning prisoner struck him a violent blow in the face, causing a wound above the eye, and knocking him to the ground. Accused also kicked him in the breast while on the ground. Accused was a stranFer to witness, and there was no reason for the ferocious assault. WORKMEN'S RAILWAY TICKETS.

Warrington Man Caught Frauding

C.L.C.

At the Borough Court on Thursday, Francis Princep, 75, Baxter-street, was fined 20s. for travelling on the Cheshire Lines between War- rington and Cadishead without paying his fare, and with intent to avoid payment. Mr. Pinfold, Liverpool, prosecuted, and ex- plained that the offence was committed in respect of workmen's tickets issued to defendant. On April sth the stationmaster at Cadishead collected a ticket belonging to a series of work- men's tickets which had been issued on March 20th, and was out of date. The following day the stationmaster kept a look out for the corre- sponding ticket, and defendant was the man who

Offered the ticket. Defendant's attention was called to the fact that the ticket was out of date, and he replied that he got the ticket on Monday. Obviously this was not true, and the reason for the out of date tickets being in de- fendant's possession was because he had only worked two days during the week commencing March 20th. Defendant paid 3s. id. for the weekly series of workmen's tickets, which was 2s. 11d, less than the ordinary fare tickets. This class of offence, which was far too rife at the present time, caused a good deal of trouble to a staff, which was short-handed. _ _ _ _

The station master _at Cadishead said defend- ant gave a false name and address. Defendant alleged that the tickets were issued with various dates on, and he had complained to the railway officials at Irlam. He denied that he gave up the tickets on the dates as alleged. The Chairman (Mr. J. Rymer Young) said de- fendant had done himself no good by giving a false name and address, and he was liable to be prosecuted for that.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
PENNY UNDER THE SCALES.

Smart Fine Imposed On Provision

Dealer.

For having unjust scales in his possession for use, Alfred Beardsall, general provision dealer, 49 and 51, Mersey-street, was, at the Borough Court onerhursday, fined 60s. Mr. A. T. Hallaway, deputy town clerk, stated that on the 3rd April, Mr. T. Malone, weights and measures inspector, visited defend- ant's shop and examined scales which were on the counter. He found a penny tied up in a piece of paper under the scales, making the weight half an ounce against the purchaser. Without the coin. the scales were one drachm. three grains against the purchaser. Mr: Malone said when he entered defendant's shop'he noticed an ounce weight on the scales. When he liftei the weight off, the scales went down. Defendant said he was not aware the scales had been used in the way alleged. He had been very ill in bed forjour months, and the business had been managed by his wife and a young girl. Mr. Hallaway did not ask for the scales to be confiscated, as they were very good ones. The Chairman, in imposing the fine stated above, described the offence as very serious.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
FOUND IN A WAGGON.

Young Man's Remarkable Escapade On Railway.

A somewhat remarkable story was unfolded to the borough magistrates on Wednesday, when a young man was charged with assaulting Edward Benson, a shunter in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Committee.

The Chief Constable, prosecuting, said he did not think accused, who appeared ill, was in a fit condition to plead. Evidence was given to the effect fhat on Tues- day night Benson was engaged marshalling a goods train on the Cheshire Lines, when the engine driver drew his attention to someone in a waggon. Proceeding to the waggon, Benson found the accused concealed, and when accused got out, he struck Benson a blow in the face. With the assistance of the fireman, accused was overpowered and handed to the police.

The Chief Constable said accused had been medically' attended, and, in the opinion of the doctor, was not responsible for his actions. His mother had promised to look after him, and under these circumstances he asked that the accused should be discharged. Accused told the magistrates that he could not sleep and he got up. He opened the bedroom window, climbed down the spouting and pro- ceeded along the lane in his stocking feet and got into the waggon. The Chief Constable said the magistrates would observe accused's- condition; and on the mother' promising to look after her son he was discharged on payment of the costs, ss. 6d.

NO REGISTRATION CARD.

A Casual First To Be Prosecuted At

Wa r rin g ton .

The first prosecution for failing to produce th,:, registration card in Warrington was heard at the Borough Court on Thursday. The accused was Frank Barrow, alias John Bailey, of no fixed abode, who was also charged with being drunk in Bridge-street on Wednesday of last week. Accused was arrested by Con- stable Hughes, and when charged by Detective- Sergeant Reddington with failing to produce his registration card he replied that he filled up the registration format Middlesbrough, but did not receive a card. The Chief Constable pointed out the penalty attached to failing to produce the registration certificate. Accused had Nen asked if he would join the Army and he replied, "No." He said they had done him for £35, and he would have nothing to do with the Army. Apparently accused had served in the Army, but long lrfore war broke out. Accused said he registered in the casual ward at Middlesbrough, but had not received a cer- tificate.

The Chief Constable said Barrow could have obtained a registration card by applying to the public authorities. A fine of ss. was imposed. and accused was advised to obtain a registration card.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
CAUGHT IN SHAFTING.

Well-known Warrington Musi- cian Accidentally Killed.

Mr. Thomas Youle, of 99, Cumberland-street, a well-known Warrington musician, niet with a tragic death at the Vernon-street Tannery, where he was employed as a " butt-striker " on Monday morning, being accidentally caught in some shafting and instantly killed. The de- ceased, who was 47 years of age, was a highly respected musician in the borough, and was one of the old school of bass players. His loss to the brass band world is 'all the more to be lamented as there are very few of his school left. He was ever ready to give his services for any charitable and deserving cause, and his genial and well mannered disposition won for him a wide circle of musical friends, and he was greatly esteemed by all who knew him. He. was one of the oldest members of the Pioneer Band, formerly old St. Paul's, and a member of the R.A.0.8. Band since its formation three years ago. For some years he figured in Mr. T. J. Down's 'Military Band. On Thursday night the Warrington Borough Coroner (Mr. S. Brighouse) held an inquest into the circumstances of Mr. Youle's death. There were present at the inquiry, Mr. Brothers (LM. Inspector of Factories), Mr. Steel, repre- senting Messrs. H. Greenall and Co.. for the Vernon-street Tannery; and Mr. Seabury, the relations of the deceased. The widow, giving evidence of identification, said her husband left home on Monday morning at 5.50, and two hours later she was informed that he had been killed. Deceased enjoyed the best of health. Dead When Found. .

George Worthington, 32, Fairclougifs-avenue, foreman at the Tannery, said some alterations had been carried out on a machine where de- ceased was working, but he had not worked on the machine for a fortnight. He was to start work on the morning of the accident. At 6.15 a.m. witness saw deceased and instructed him to fix two iron sets to the machine to steady It. Witness then left him and went to another part of the works. The machine was not at that time in motion, but near deceased, about, four yards awair on his right, was a belting attached to the driving shafting turning a wooden drum, and this was in motion. At 6.30 witness noticed that the drum had stopped, and he went to see the cause. He noticed that the belt was off the driving shaft, and was examining it, when he saw de- ceased lying in the shafting of the drum. His legs were in the air. his head being about six inches from the driving pulley. He was apparently dead. In reply to the Factory Inspector, witness said he knew of no reason ,for his crossing the shaft- ing. His clothing was-wrapped round the shaft- ing.

By Mr. Steel: Deceased had nothing to do with the drums which were running. Deceased was a very steady and reliable man. By a Juryman:. Youle Was in no way respon- siblg for the oiling of the drums. The jury returned a verdict that deceased' met his death accidentally.

On behalf of the TaTming Co.. Mr. Steel ex- pressed sympathy with the widow in her bereavn- ment. Deceased, he said, had been in the firm's emnlov over 22 rears. and during the whole of that time they found him always a steady and hardworking man.

CHILD'S DEATH FROM BURNS.

A Warrington Coroner's jury on Thursday night returned a verdict that Alice Green, two- year-old daughter of Alfred abd Susan Green, of 3, Manley-street, died from burns accidentally received.

The evidence 'showed that on April 15th the mother placed the child, in front of the kitchen fire and left the room for a few moments. On returning she found the child badly burned about the legs, while her nightdress was smouldering. Castor oil having been applied to tho burns the child was removed to the Infirmary, but, after receiving tieatment, was taken back home. A.s the child's cc.ndition be 31133 worse she was again taken to the Infirmary, and re- mained there. until death took plac.-: on Monday from septic pneumonia, following burns.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
A NON-SMOKING COMPARTMENT.

When a youth named Stanley Lagar, of 110, Knutsford-road, claimed a week's wages in lieu of notice, at the Munitions Court on Monday, the manager of the works elicited from him the admission that there was a regulation in the works that anyone found smoking would be subject to instant dismissal. Although Lagar denied that he had been warned about smoking three times on the day of his dis- missal, the wanager asserted that that was a fact.

On Legar's claim being disallowed, the man- ager remarked that he thought that in addition to refusing the claim the, Tribunal ought to discourage in a strict manner such vexatious claims, but the Tribunal refrained from saying anything further to Lagar.

By Doctor's Orders.

• "You must drink hot water with your whisky," the doctor told his patient; "other- wise you mustn't take it at all."

"Bit how shall I get -hot water?" the patient queried plaintively. "My wife won't let me have it for the whisky toddy." Tell her you want it to shave," the doctor said, and took his departure. The next day the doctor called and asked the wife how the patient was. **He's gone raving mad," his wife replied. "He shaves every 10 minutes."

FOOTBALL.

ASSOCIATION.

Military Beat Civilians By A

Goal.

FAST ATTACKS.

About 500 spectators including.a good sprink- ling of soldiers mo6tly officers and men of the 15th lOng's (Liverperl) Regiment witnessed a match in aid of the Mayor's War Fund, be- tween the King's and Warrington T,)wn, at Hood-lane on Saturday. The .ground was rather heavy, but, notwith- standing, the game was very fast throughout. The Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) kicked-off, the visitors having won the toss,. and &Acted to play with the wind at their backs. Warring- ton started with nine men, Kite being one of the absentees. They soon put in an appearance, however, and immediately following good work by the King's forwards, Kite was • beaten with a cross shot. Cookson, however, had dropped back into the goal mouth, and to the amuse- ment of all, he caught the ball with the dexterity, of a first class "goalie," and so pre- vented—at least for a while—a goal. Of course, a penalty was at once. awarded the visitors, and Kite was beaten with a fast, high .shot. This -happened after about three Minutes' play. Fromthe restart the King's were soon attack- ing. The home defence, holi'eier, proved safe, and good combined work by Gregson and Hlidge, saw the Town invade the visitors' -quarters. A good centre • was made, and Aspinall, on 'receiving, forged between the full- backs and left Croft helpless with a fine shot.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
The Town Enlivened.

Enlivened by this success, War-rington at- tacked hotly, and again made progress by means of the right-wing, and Young had to kick be- hind for safety. The corner-kick was cleared. following which the King's broke away, but Leah drove them back with a hard kick. Again they came, and this time forced a corner-kick, but the defence proved safe, and danger was averted. Warrington dashed away to the other end, but the ball was put behind, following which the visitors invadeil the home territory

and forced Kite to handle. A minute later, smart work by Aspinall saw Croft in trouble, and, after fumbling, he cleared in the luckieit fashion. Warrington again attacked, but off- side spoiled t''he movement. Pilkington received when well placed, and he put in a hard, straight, drive. Kite got down and cleared easily, following which Owen broke away, and -made a nice run along touch before the ball rolled out of play. Shortly afterwards Kendrick was only inches wide with a fast shot, while Owen was stopped when making one of his characteristic runs along the line. The home forwards now combined, and Aspinall was only just wide with a nice shot sent in while on the run. Half-time :—Warring- ton Town, 1 goal; 15th King's Regiment, 1 goal. The second half had a sensational opening. Straight from the kick-off the King's broke away, and before the homesters realised their position Rigby had got through and beaten Kite easily. It was a remarkable goal, only about three or four players having touched the ball. Roused by this'reverse,. the Town attacked hotly, and Owen made another run along the line, but lost the ball. The King's now came away, but they got offside, and, from the free- kick, Illidge received, and after a good run, had a shot at goal, but the ball rolled outside.

BOWLING.

NOTES ON OUR TEAMS.

From the score sheet it will be seen that in the first round of the Lancashire County Amateur Association Championship, held on Saturday, of the three competing Warrington teams, only the Cemetery Hotel came out on top, though I am happy to say that Rylands's workmen, who only went down by six points, have successfully gained permission to replay the game against West Leigh Conservatives. It is, therefore, pos- sible that two Warrington teams, at least, will go into the second, round.

Rylands's workmen are keen and able, bowlers, and with only six points between them and their opponents last week, we may be sure that they will put forth every effort to win.

Bull's Head were completely outclassed by 'Ellesmere (Leigh), the latter team putting up 232 points against 176. Only three Bull's Head players out of the twelve gained the requisite 21. These were Messrs. A. Randles, T. Hackett and T. Rigby. The first two showed superiority from the start, and gradually extended the difference between themselves and , their opponents. until finally Randles-ran home with 10 points in hand and Hackett with 9.

Trt the case of T. Rigby, however, things were not so rosy with him in the earlier pasts of the game; in fact, he looked- like a loser for some time. Nearly always the woods were narrow, ami it was points of inches between' them. His opponent, J. Grundy, had scaled ten points while he himself only had one, but he had a few successes and brought the points up to 8-11. His opponent, however, then forged ahead, obtaining another three points before Mr. Rigby could score another one. The latter stuck man- fully to the game, and although four behind when his contestant only wanted one to make him winner, he kept a cool head, a steady hand, and a sure aim, and, making a run of five, gained the victory.

I was rather disapnointed in the majority of the scores. Mr. J. Isherwood onlv got six noints. Mr. A. Regan twelve. and Mr. G. Chamberlain ten. Mr. George Shaw nut up a gallant fight against P. Ratcliffe, and though well heater for the preater part of the game. was ultimately only deprived of victory by two points.

The Cemetery Hotel fairly romped over the Ship Hotel, all but two of the -players gaining the full CeO7V and these gentlemen—s. Plant and H. Pat,'lllopp—only being short of three noints each. C. .Tordan soundly beat G. Nelson by 18 points. the next heaviest nunishment being given C. Caldwell by A. Forster, the latter only aiinwine. Caldwell to get six. F. Dale defeated W. Wragg in identical fashion.

WARRINGTON WORKS AMATEUR ASSOCIATION.

War Fund Handicap.

About 120 games were played in the first stages of the War Fund Handicap at the Horse and Jockey Inn, Winwick-road, on Saturday. Though the green has been seen in better Con- dition, play was very enioyable indeed. With the shortage 'of labour green-masters are finding it most difficult to keep their swards up to their established reputation. The competition is still open, and it is advisable that intending competitiors should make application at once. No time-limit will be .allowed competitors.

The winners on Saturday were :—T. Drew (L.C.), G. Caddick (P. & K.), W. Caddick (R.M.), W. Massey (R.M.), W. Mullholland (C.A.), T. Boardman (W.) A. Savage' fF.S.T.), W. Bridge (CIA.), T. ===== 0002601_19160513_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
Pace Stilt Hot

Play was now being waged at a very hot pace, first one end and then the other being visited. During an attack by the .visiting for- wards Kite was forced to save twice in as many minutes, while at the other end Owen forced a corner. He dropped the ball into the goal- mouth with perfect judgment, and it was headed in. Many onlookers thought Croft was beaten, but he jumped up and fisted it out in excellent style. Warrington came again, how- ever, and Owen forced another corner, but the ball went outside, whilst, from a.centre by the. same player, Aspinall headed over. Give-and- take was now the order to the end of the game, the defence on both sides proving too strong for the attack, and nothing further was recorded.

Result :-15th King's (Liverpool) Regiment, 2 goals; Warrington Town, 1 goal. Teams:—

15th King's: Croft (Huyton Quarry); Young (Bolton Wanderers), Woods (Darwen); Jones (Manchester City), Rigby (Central), Abrams (Chelsea), Pownell (Port Sunlight); Kendrick (Warrington), Pilkington (Darwen), Rigby (Manchester City), Wallace (Bootle). Warrington Town: Kite; Leah, Cookson; Ashworth, Whitfield, Millington; Gregson, Illidge, .Aspinall, Taylor, Owen. Great interest was taken in the performance of Kendrick, a Warrington lad, who has played with the Sacred Heart and the Elmwood Con- gregational Clubs, and it may be said that since joining the Army he has greatly improved his abilities on the football field, much to the satisfaction of some of his old comrades, who were amongst the spectators, and they were liberal in their appreciation of any smart play shown by him during the course of the game.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT MEDAL COMPETITION.

FINAL TIE.

On Saturday the Widnes Athletic and Win- wick Rovers, the finalists in the above competi- tion, met on the Atherton Quay ground, the conditions being suitable for a hard and fast game. A good crowd of spectators were present when the following players lined out:— Widnes Athletic: Humphries; Harrison and Jim Murty; Illidge, Joe Murty and Brown; Melia, Hill, Cleaver, Broome and Gregory. Winwick Rovers: Hollas; Riley and Sherlock; Morse, Sherlock and Simms Hodgson, Harri- son, Gleave, Gee and Anderton.

Linesmen, Mr. Hinds (Warrington Central), Mr. Hatton (Sankey Athletic). _

Referee, Mr. G. i3amber. The Rovers won the toss and defended the goal nearest the river. The Athletic set the ball rolling, and with the wind in their favour they were soon attacking. Hill shot over the bar. Then followed a spell of keen and interesting midfield play. Broome and Gregory were enabled to lead another attack on the Ravers' goal, and this was repulsed by the fine defensive play of Riley, which led to a visit to the Athletic quarters without result. The Athletic were soon on the offensive again., and Hill scored an offside goal. The free-kick enabled the Rovers to make tracks to their opponents' goal, but Harrison was on the alert and cleared with a big kick. The Athletic forwards showing good combinatioa, became very dangerous, a good centre by Hill being smartly converted by Cleaver, who com- pletely beat Hollas. After this reverse the Rovers increased their efforts, but were met 133, a stout defence, and up to the interval play was in midfield, the scores being :—Athletic, 1 goal; Rovers, nil.

On resuming the Rovers had the assistance af the wind, and pressed strongly, but the Athletic defence did not waver. A visit was paid to the Rovers' eitadel, but was easily 'repulsed by their defenders, and another attack on' the—Athletic goal resulted in Gee equalising with a fine shot. From the centre-kick the game was very keenly and strenuously contested, the ball travelling quickly from end to end. A strong attack by the Athletic was repulsed by the tactics of the Rovers' defence, and then the Athletic goal was subjected to a strong assault, being eventually captured. The remainder of the game was evenly contested, and chiefly in midfield. At the call of time the scores being:—Widnes Athletic, I goal; Winwick Rovers, 1 goal. An extra half-hour was decided upoili, and hostilities soon recommenced. Both teams were energetic in their play, but on every oecasion the defence proved too good for the attacks. Humphries and Hollas, in their respective goals, showed good.judgment in their work, and were' well supported by their backs., At the conclusion of this period no definite 'decision had been arrived at.

Another extra twenty minutes was -ntered upon, and during this stage of the contest it became evident that the strain was telling on the players, as some of their final attempts were very weak, and after a struggle o two and a half hours the game was ;eft drawn, the scores being one goal each.

Medical.

THE TRIBUNALS' DECISIONS.

MATTERS OF BUSINESS.

Amusement Proprietor To Sell Up Before Going.

Mostly matters of business, with here and there a human note, .were heard at the Tribunal at the Town Hall on Wednesday afternoon.' An amusement proprietor, who said he had ,R5OO worth Of stock in side-shows, vans, horses, &c., asked for time in order that he might sell them. His brother, who was in the Army, used to help him. He (the applicant) was quite willing to join the Army. All he wanted was time to clear up his affairs. s His stock was now at Crewe.—Exemption until the end of June was granted. There was a human note in the application of a young man who is the sole support of his aged father and mother. The father is an invalid, and about a fortnikht ago had a re- lapse, the doctor stating that any shock might prove fatal to him. - Dr. Joseph: Would it be a shock if you joined the Army F—lt would, sir. On being told that he would be granted con- ditional exemption, Mr: A. T. Hallaway (tae clerk) remarked: "You can go out smiling now !"

_ Applicant Yes; lam sure my father will be pleased when he hears the -result. If it had been 'otherwise I dared not have told him.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
MINDING HIS SOLDIER-FATHER'S BUSINESS.

A hardware hawker, who is also carrying on hi father's coal business while that gentleman is serving in the National Reserve, again ap- pealed, his case having previously been ad- journed in order that he might dispose of his business. Although he had not advertised he had tried to sell it to some friends, who had since been called up themselves., • In exempting him for a further two months, the Chairman said: "That is -final. It is pro- bable that your father may be home by then."

===== 0002601_19160513_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
BUTCHER EXEMPTED.

A butcher, who is 39 years of age, has two children, three shops, and no male employes, was ,conditionally exempted. I EX-ORGAN GRINDER'S SON.

Conditional 4xemption was granted to a single man, who has had a brother killed in France, and has another brother serving in the Army. His father is nearly blind and used to go about with a street organ.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTRICIAN'S APPLICATION ADJOURNED.

Through Mr. T. S. Steel, an electrical engineer and contractor, who has been in busi- ness on his own account for seven years, ap- plied. Since the outbreak of war seven em- ployes had joined the Army, he said, and he had now twelve persons in his employ—six men, four apprentices and two lady laookkeepers. He could do with half-a-dozen more employes, for he had a great deal of work on. Five out of the six men had attested and he was willing to release two or three men. He was responsible for the maintenance of the electrical plant of several works engaged on munitions and had applied for war badges both for himself and some of his men. The application was adjourned for a month to see whether war badges are granted.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
THRESHING MACHINE MAN.

Two months' exemption was allowed a feeder employed by. Mr. Walter Fitter, threshing machine proprietor. Mr. Fitter was to have appeared last week and he now explained that he was precluded from fulfilling the engage- ment because of "tyro trouble" with a motor car in the country. He wanted this man very badly, as it was most difficult to get one in .his place. POSTPONING THE INEVITABLE.

"I recognise he will have to go, but I want to put off the inevitable day as long as pos- sible," observed a boot repairer, in asking for a temporary exemption for his apprentice. It was practically impossible to get men, despite what were, for the trade, unique inducements offered. He considered that the shodding of children's boots was work of national import- ance, and some Tribunals, notably that of Salford, had held it so. Dr. G. W. Joseph (chairman) : We do not want to know about any other Tribunal. We have sufficient confidence in ourselves.

Exempted until 10th June. Eight glassmakers and a clerk working for Messrs. Robinson and Co. were conditionally exempted. THE PAPER SHORTAGE.

Messrs. Pochiu and Co. gained an exemption until the end of May for a grinder employed on material used in paper-making and also for sewage precipitants. The manager explained that there was a great shortage of paper.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
SUCCESSFUL APPEALS FOR CONDITIONAL EXEMPTION.

The Borough Tribunal again sat On Thursday afternoon, Dr. Joseph presiding. A large num- ber of appeals came under review, and a fair number of appellants succeeded in obtaining conditional exemption. IN BUSINESS.

A builder and contractor, single, aged 28, applied for absolute exemption on business grounds. He said he had been in business on his own account since August last, previous to which he managed his grandfather's business. He had several contracts -to complete. The Tribunal granted appellant one month's exemp. tion.

Appellant: I suppose I can appeal again? Dr. Joseph: You cannot appeal here. It is final so far as we are concerned.

Appellant was supplied with an appeal form.

Two months' exemption was granteila painter, single, aged 34, in a large way of business. He had a considerable amount of work on hand, and there was only his father, himself and a labourer. Four men had left him to join the Army. Painting work was essentially summer work. He thought he would be able to make satisfactory "arrangements in respect of his business if he had three months' exemption. He had no desire to shirk his obligations.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
TOBACCONIST'S CLAIM.

" In NI arrington it is the custom for tobacco- nist businesses to be managed by young•ladies," said the Chairman, in granting a tobacconist in business on his own account three months' ex- emption in order to make the best arrangements he could. The appellant said be had been in business for nine years, and if he had to go he was afraid the business would *Piave to be closed. He employed a young lady assistant.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER SONS IN THE ARMY.

A • nineteen-year-old youth was granted six months' exemption on domestic grounds. The mother of the appellant said she had two other sons in the Army.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
REDRESSED FINANCIAL HARDSHIP.

A cinema operator applied for absolute exemp- tion on the domestic grounds that he was the sole support of his mother. He said his mother had advertised for lodgers, but there was such a scarcity that she could not get any. Appellant was given one month's extensions the Chairman informing him that if there was any financial hardship it would be redressed by the new Act pending. A DIFFICULT POINT.

A married painter and decorator, aged 25, appealed on domestic and business grounds. He had been in business on his own since November. 1913. He employed one man, aged 41. The Tribunal adjourned the appeal, the Chairman stating that the Tribunal did not know what legislation would be passed in respect of relief for such cases.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
CONDITIONAL EXEMPTIONS.

Mr. R. Knight, 21, Orford-lane, master clog maker, applied for the conditional exemption of a clogger. He held the Warrington Guardians' contract. Conditional exemption was granted. Similar exemption was also granted a milk dealer, a married man with a wife and five chil- dren ; and a baker aged 33. The latter appealed on behalf of an indentured apprentice, aged Is, and the Tribunal granted exemption till October, when his indentures would be completed.

• CARTING DIFFICULTIES. Messrs. Milling and Co. appealed on behalf of

a motor van driver, aged 37.—Three months' exemption. _ _

Mr: Browne presented the appeal of Messrs. Caldwell and Sons, carting contractors, on be- half of one of the sons of the principal of the firm. The man was employed as a carter, and he had been previously put back a month. He was chiefly employed in carting Government work. Mr. Browne laid stress on the fact that there was a great shortage of labour, and one firm for whom his clients did carting had ten to fourteen horses idle.—The appeal was refused. Messrs. Naylor and Co., toffee manufacturers, represented by Mr. Browne, appealed for the renewal of exemption certificates of a clerk and a traveller-carter. Mr. Browne said Messrs. Naylor had four horses grazing in the fields be- cause they had no drivers to work them. They had given notice of appeal for another man, who was called up on May 29th, and if the Tribunal would grant conditional exemption to the two men he was now appealing for he would abandon the other appeal.—The Tribunal granted the clerk two months' exemption, but in the case of the traveller, the Tribunal adjourned it to enable him and the other man to be appealed for to be medically examined. Mr. Browne, on behalf of Messrs. Crompton and Shawcross, coal merchants, appealed for the exemption of a married foreman carter employed at the Dallam coal yard. Mr. Browne said the man had supervision over 4,000 waggons passing through Warrington, and he was engaged in tracing missing waggons on the railway. He carted coal to Corporation and other works Appellant had four brothers in the Army. Be- fore the war the firm employed five men, but they had gone to the Front. He contended that it was a righteous appeal for exemption. Dr. Joseph: Conditional exemption is granted. We are righteous. you see. Mr. Browne: You are just, you are righteous for once, although you may be aspersed.

THE ATTRACTION OF OVERTIME. A remarkable statement was made by a master bricksetter and contractor in appealing for one of his workmen. He said he paid one man lid, per hour, but he went into munition works for 101 d. per hour. He could not give any reason for this, except the attraction of overtime and war bonus.

Captain Mathams : Are you going to appeal? Employer: No, I cannot. I have been rejected. (Laughter.)

The workman was granted conditional exemp- tion.

LOCAL MANAGER AND FOREMAN.

Messrs. Johnson and Co., Liverpool, applied for the exemption of the manager of their Wel- rington shop and the works foreman. A repre- sentative of the firm said they were the only plasterers in Warrington at present. Thq manager was granted conditional exemption and the foreman was put back for three months. A FRUITERER'S APPLICATIONS.

Mr. G. Miller, wholesale and retail fruiterer, appealed on behalf of a youth, 18 years old, who also appealed on domestic grounds. The father of .the youth caused some amuse- ment when asked how many sons he had, by saying he could not call them all to mind. At first he said he had eight, then six, and eventually a friend whispered into his ear that he had seven. Three sons had been with the Forces. One had been missing for some time, and another had been wounded and discharged as medically un- fit. Mr. Miller's appeal was disallowed, but appellant was exempted for six months on domestic grounds. Mr. Miller had also an appeal for another youth aged 18, and Alderman Tinnion asked if he insisted upon a youth of 18. Mr. Miller said he did not want To insist upon anything. The appeal was refused.

Mr. Miller was suecessful in obtaining con- ditional exemption for his salesman, aged 28 and married.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
CHANCING OCCUPATIONS.

Wholesale Butcher's Appeal For 'Head

Slaughter.

A remarkable statement was made by Mr. Shaw, wholesale butcher, of Orford-lane, at yesterday's sitting of the Tribunal. He was appealing for the exeMption of his head slaugh- terman, and he said since the war broke out he had lost three slaughtermen, two of whom were now working in tanneries, and another in a tube works. He thought they had gone on this work to escape service.

Captain gathams : Have any of these men gone since last August? Appellant: I think one man has. Appellant said he supplied every butcher in Warrington, also Lord Derby War Hospital' and the Barracks Hospital. He killed 60 beasts and 120 sheep weekly. In a few weeks' time he would kill 200 sheep.

Four months' exemption was granted, and Mr. Shaw said in the meantime he would try to get. someone to fill the man's place.

LIFE'S SAVINGS INVESTED.

Mr. C. H. Unsworth supported an appeal on behalf of a master baker aged 24, and single, who, he said, supplied foodstuff to the com- munity to the extent of £126 per week, and it was a work of national interest. Appellant employed ten females, two men over military age, and three boys. One brother who was in the business had joined the Navy. He had in- vested his life's savings to the extent of £6OO. He purchased the business two years ago. He baked solid articles of food and not fancies. He had his father and mother to support.—The Tribunal adjourned their decision for a week.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTRAORDINARILY LUCKY!

Mr. Wm. Stringer, wholesale baker, 139, Mersey-street, appealed on behalf of his single son, aged 22. Appellant said he had another son aged 25, single, in the business, who had been exempted, and also another single employe. Dr. Joseph: You have been extraordinarily lucky to keep three single men under 25. Applicant: What can I do? You cannot get any other.

The Chairman: This is a time to make sacri- fices. We cannot grant exemption in this case. We don't know what the military will do in regard to the others.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY OFFICER'S OFFER.

The appeal of Mr. B. Cross, butcher, for his son. aged 20, was refused.

The Clerk said appellant had asked that his son should be put back to the last group, and he was now called up. Was he satisfied? Mr. Cross said he could not fill his son's place. He, himself, was not able to do the wcrk as he used to do.

Captain Mathams said he thought he could find appellant a butcher discharged from the '.S.C.

Mr. Cross said there was nothing else for him hut to close down if his son was taken.

DEAF, DUMB AND LAME EMPLOYES.

Messrs. Greenhalgh and Co., Packing case manufacturers, applied for the exemption of two single packing case makers, aged 22 and 23 -espectively. The employer stated that out of six packing case makers left, four were deaf, dumb a nil lame. The appeal of the youngest man was refused, and the other was granted a month's exemption.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
FARMER'S APPEAL ADJOURNED.

The Tribunal adjourned the appeals of Mr. T. Ford, farmer, Latchford, for his two sons, aged 24 and 19, both single. He had one son in the Army. He farmed 130 acres. TRIBUNAL TO VISIT WORKS.

The United Velvet Cutters' Association appealed for the exemption of a velvet cutter and tooler aged 21. Mr. Steel, for the firm, said the man was in charge of a room where there were 43 women employed, The firm had only four toolers employed who were under 41. They had advertised for toolers, but had failed to get any replies. The ease was adjourned, the Chairman inti- mating that the Tribunal would like to visit the works.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
BAKER AND POTATO SCARCITY.

Mr. James Harding, master baker, appealed for the exemption of his son aged 21, and single. He said his son helped in the bakeho'qse and went out with :Lynn. Appellant said he had a man aged 40 with six children who was to be called up, hut he wolild not appeal for him.

The Clerk considered he was the man who should be appealed for. Appellant said the man told him some months ago that he did not want anyone to appeal for him. Appellant stated that he was using more sacks of flour now, because of the scarcity of potatoes. The, appeal was refused, and appellant described it as a hardship.

The Chairman: You will be able to appeal for the man of 40, who ought to be kept rather than the man of 21.

Applicant said a baker when he had done baking would not take a van out. He could not get bakers for "love or money."

BAKING LESSONS WANTED.

A master baker, 32, was given conditional exemption. He said he had no other man ia the business, and he baked 15 sacks of flour entirely on his own. The Clerk: You will have to give lessons as to how you bake 15 sacks on your own. Appellant: You had better come and see me.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
DESERTS!

Mr. Shaw, athletic outfiteer, alipealed for an asistant aged 38. The Clerk: You were here on 3rd of March and got two months. Mr. Shaw: Yes, not in gaol! The Clerk: Some of us don't get our deserts. (Laughter.) Mr. Shaw: We have all done something in our time to deserve it.

The Clerk: You are speaking for yourself. (Laughter‘) The appeal was adjourned to enable the man to be medically examined. WANTS HOME SERVICE.

An employe in the General Post Office, aged 34, and married, was granted three months' exemption on domestic grounds. Appellant asked if he could be put on home service.

The Chairman said that was a question for the military. ' Appellant agreed to be medically examined.

ONLY WORKER LEFT.

Mr. T. J. Boswell, of Golborne-street, appealed for a sheet metal worker, aged 18, and singles He said he was the only employe he had. A man between 40 and 50 years of ago left him to join the National Reserve. The Chairman: He was the man who ought to have stayed. Appellant said it was impossible to get.sheet metal workers. Two months' exemption was given.

1 boilerman, aged 31, employed by the War- rington Products Co., who appealed for him, was given conditional exemption.

BUSINESS CLOSED DOWN.

Mr. Hudson, agricultural ironmonger,. was granted conditional exemption for one of his employes. He said he was releasing his tin- smith, who was to be called up, and this would mean the closing down of this business.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH AND HATTON.

Dearth Of Labour in Cheshire

Districts.

The proceedings at the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Appeals Tribunal, held at the Dutton Workhouse on: Tuesday afternoon, were very dull. Some thirty-three appeals came under review, the bulk of them being in respect of agricultural workers. The Clerk (Mr. W. E. Hough) intimated that he had received from the County Appeals Tribunal the decisions in respect to appeals made by appellants who had been before that (Runcorn Rural) Tribunal.

The Chairman (Mr. Linaker, sen.) said in the bulk of the cases the decision of the Run- corn Rural Tribunal had been upheld by the County Tribunal. ' A Newton-by-Daresbury farm bailiff was granted exemption till September Ist. He was the only regular hand on a farm of 56 acres. A previous farm bailiff who had been exempted had been dismissed.

A jobbing gardener, belonging to Stockton Heath, appealed on the ground of physical infirmity, deafness and head noises. Appellant said he was aged 41 and single. After putting a number of questions to the appellant, the Tribunal agreed that he was not a fit man for the Army and he was advised to see the medical officer to the military authorities and obtain the necessary medical form of rejection.

A contractor in a large way of business appealed for, the exemption of a Warrington joiner. He said the man was at present en- gaged on important work at controlled works. —The appeal was refused. Hatton Widower and His Children.

A widower with four children, the eldest aged nine, appealed on domestic grounds. He said he was a garden labourer at Hatton and in receipt of 225. 2d. per week wages. He had a housekeeper and paid is. 6d. rent. He de- sired to know what allowance would be made for his children and housekeeper. He was informed that ss. each would be allowed for his four children, but nothing in respect of his housekeeper.—Appellant was exempted till July Ist in order to make arrangements for the welfare of his children. Preston Brook Farmer Succeeds.

A Preston Brook farmer, appealing for his son, who acted as stockman on a farm of 250 acres, said he had four men at present on the farm. ", I want six more if I can get hold of them," he declared. He would have to go himself if they took any more men from him. —Conditional exemption was granted. Gardener's Appeal.

Supporting an appeal for exemption of a gardener, a well-known Warrington gentleman and a director of one of the borough's largest concerns, said his family and himself were doing their duty as true citizens in the present crisis. The gardener was producing foodstuffs for two families thus reducing the need for obtaining such supplies elsewhere. His own two sons and son-in-law were serving with the Forces. Owing to his multifarious duties, both business and public, he could not possibly give the time and attention to a garden of two acres in extent. He had dismissed his chauf- feur because he had declined to enlist.—The Tribunal granted exemption till July Ist. The Food Supply.

- - - A Keckwi2k farmer, appealing for the exemp- tion of a teamsman, said farmers had no men to look after horses. Farmers had been ap- pealed to to increase the food supply of the country, but they could not do it unless they had male labour.—Conditional exemption was granted.

===== 0002601_19160513_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
FRODSIIAM FISH DEALER'S FURTHER EXTENSION.

There- was only one local appeal before the County Tribunal held at Chester Castle on Tuesday. A Frodsham fish, game and poultry dealer, for whom Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, Warrington, appeared, appealed against the decision of the local authority, who had granted him exemption until the Ist of this month. It was stated that he purchased the business two years ago. He borrowed part of the money and had only repaid Beso. He paid a yearly rental of. £25 and rates and taxes. He had one man, working for him and two .boys. The man wa's married and his group had been called up. He supplied the residents in an extensive district, and his was the only shop of its kind in a radius of ten miles. He also supplied a local military hospital. Three days after entering his appeal he was taken ill with pneumonia, which was followed by sciatica and lumbago, and he had only• got out of bed the previous day. During his illness his brother- in-law had had to make frequent journeys from Alderley Edge to supervise, as well as he could, the appellant's business, but he sustained a considerable loss. In answer to Captain Rigby (military repre- sentative) the appellant stated that the man he employed could not manage the business in his (the appellant's) absence. The exemption was extended until :;he Ist of June..
