STAGE AND KINEMA.

From Monday onwards a new stock turn in the person of a tax collector will perform at the Royal Court Theatre, Palace and Hippo- drome, and all the cinema houses in the borough. Licensed to appear on the initiative of Mr. McKenna, on. the 2d. seats and under he will demand from patrons id. extra, id. on the 6d. seats, and 2d. on the Is. seats.

The proprietors are to get nothing out of this extra money, except the trouble of seeing that it is collected. Application has been made that each management shall submit a certified return of receipts and pay the tax on that basis, but permission has not yet come to hand. Until this arrives special Government ,tickets will be used. Royal Court. - -

Lirge audiences have been very much inter- ested in "Home Once More," a piny written and produced by 'Miss Emma Litchfield. It is well above the average drama, and I could well appreciate the fact Miss Litcbfiqd mentioned to me that the quickest time sue ever wrote a pla- in was three months. The situations which arise are too clever and subtle to have been dashed off with the speed one signs an attesta- tion form.

"This is my tenth play," Miss Litchfield added, "and I am very proud of the fact that I have not yet written a failure. Touch wood !" —and she put one hand on her head and the other on her chair. It was vivaciousness rather surprising, after her sombre yet beautiful ren- dering of a misunderstood, wronged and good woman, in the character of Mary Lorraine. Yet it was characteristic, as for instance, "I come of a very old theatrical family. My great or great-great grandmother was at Covent Garden, and played the ' heavy ' leading lady with Mrs. Siddons. lam very proud of that fact, although as the Irishman said of the potatoes, they are underground."

Miss Litchfield is passlcinrtfily fond ••of.: the footlights. It .is not just business, with .her. "If I .had a .thousand athinykt-c (;; at the chocolate boy's wages., I in t,i,c101.:,;,_ ci), would not retire," she declared. "it is wonder- ful what a hold it gets on one." Of course, a great deal depends upon one's talent. And Miss Litchfield has that in a Marked degree. Her naturalness, gains its effect always.

A small and talented company surround her. They are quite a happy family—two married couples among them, these being Mr. S. P. Goodyer-Kettley and Miss Ena Newham, and Mr. Lionel Balmont and Miss Lillie Livesey.

Possessed of a distinguished and intellectual appearance, Mr. Goodyer-Rettley has played in many big successes with Martin Harvey, and is also reputed to be one of the best Sherlock Holmes we have. He was largely instrumental in popularising Holmes in America and Australia. I remember him more particularly in "The Speckled Band." I may add that he did not have charge of the snakes. "No thanks," be 'protested, when I asked him about it.

Miss Newham, who is playing the " heavy " for the first time, has a good Shakespearian record, but for some time she has been leading lady for Miss Litchfield. Patrons of the Royal Court will no doubt remember her in "His Indian Wife" and "A Man's Best Pal."

The authors of "Mary from Tipperary " Miss Henrietta Schrier and Mr. Lodge-Percy— are to present "The Blind Girl's Ropry " next week. It is to be presented by an excellent com- pany, and, knowing what we do of the authors, we may be sure it will be full of interest.

Ili opodrome. The programme is particularly good this week, and, perhaps, no one would have objected if the tax had started already. The turns are worth an extra.

We have been lucky to get Charlie Manny and Bob Roberts, the Messenger Boys from Broadway. Only about a month ago they were at a command performance at Buckingharh Palace. Sir Douglas Haig was, sitting near the King, and, being Americans, the Messenger Boys neutralised for the time being the effects of war on our trusted Commander-in-Chief and Sovereign.

These Boys are splendid fellows. Their worth as appraised in London may be ,judged from the fact that they have appeared in five con- secutive revues at the Alhambra Theatre, and in the summer are to join up in "Bing Boys."

A characteristic is their originality. Four out of five songs are of their own creation, and they have no one knows 'how many to their ,credit. They never sing the same songs in repeat visits to a town. They are always .up to something new. While Charlie stays at home and worries out the witty verses, Bob the Nigger (having washed) extemporises in tunes while he plays golf. I don't know what his handicap is in that game, but he is a plus man on the stage.

Another striking personality at the Palace this week is Zakaree Ermakov, the Russian, who effects some remarkable feats of arms—rifle, sword, battleaxe and spear. His lecture on his experiences in the Russian Secret Service have been most valuable.

It was through his skill in the use of arms that he weaned his way into the confidence of foreign officers, including the Crown Prince of Germany, whom he describes as "a tall, slimy, young fop, with a waist like a grasshopper—he wears stays—all eyes, nose and lips.

M. Ermakov always speaks vividly and flu- ently. His skill in languages enjoins respect. English, German, French and Turkish are home languages for him. In travelling about he has had several narrow escapes from death, apart from the gruesome experience of being arrested in Germany as a spy and enclosed in a prison for four months uncharged, and then released. But of these secret service incidents he mainly refrained from speaking.

In Russia a train he was travelling in has been held up by bandits, and a bomb was thrown at a chief of police with whom he was conversing, and exploded a couple of yards away, killing eleven men.

About three years ago, when lie was on a visit to a hall in Bradford, then managed by Mr. W. A. Jackson, of this house, an accident resulted in a bullet passing through the muscle of one of his arms. Mr. Jackson bandaged it up, and he went through his performance with an anxious wife and doctor waiting in the wings. ,

Some capital turns are down for next week. They include Cullen and Carthy with their Wee Fellow. They are well-known comedians, who are bringing with them their brilliant cross- talk. A. D. Robbins, who can do more tricks with a bicycle than anyone else, is also due, as allo are the Sultan Bros., comedy acrobats and burlesque boxers, who have been the hit of the pantomime season at the Theatre Royal, Man- chester. The Kinemas.

Star K nema. Strong drama has been the fare at the Star during the week. "Mr. Lynden at Liberty" on Monday Tuesday and Wednesday. "The Fatal Card," a \famous Players' production on Thurs- day, Friday and Saturday. Both were excel- lent pictures of dramatic interest..

On Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday next week an adaptation of the charming story from the pen .of " Ouida," entitled "Two Little Wooden Shoes" will surely prove a great attrac- tion.

A special " star " attraction for the latter part of the week will he a magnificent photo- play from the classic story written by John Luther Long (the opera is by Puccini) and pro- duced for the screen by the famous Players' Co., entitled "Madame Butterfly."

The Picture House. Excellent programmes have been screened at the Picture House, Winmarleigh-street, this week, the main items being "A Model Wife" and "Out of the Ashes." This last is still on view.

Next week we are to have something both exciting and artistic, these including " Playing for ITigh Stakes" and "The Dawn fond."

PENKETH.

Private Wi Ison.—Private Albert Ernest Wil- son, R.A.M.C., who has been absent from Eng- land for twelve months, has returned on a short well-earned furlough to his home in Bank Gardens. Probably before these lines are in print he will be away again tending to our wounded war-worn heroes. Life with him dur- ing the past year has been a continual journey from one end of the Mediterranean to the other on a hospital ship. At present he looks and feels in the pink of condition. May he keep so.

Band of Hope Concert.—On 'Tuesday evening the Band of Hope in connection with St. Mary's Church and Mission held their annual concert in the Infants' Schoolroom, Penketh. There was a large attendance, and the children got through a long and interesting programme with great credit to themselves and their trainers, Mrs. E. Dumbill and Mrs. Dvrerryhouse. Rev. .T. R. Jones presided, and Mr. D. G. Griffiths added a short speech. Mr., Stevenson was the accom- panist.

New Rates.—The overseers (Mes§rs4 James Ditchfield, J. G. Withinshaw and Thomas Davies) have received the sanction of Messrs. James B. Aitken and Charles E. Parker (two of His Majesty's Justices of the Peace for the county) to the levying of poor and special rates on the township at 2.5. 10d. and 4d. in the S respectively for the half-year ending September 30th next. These amounts are the same as last half-year.

FOR OUR CAPTURED KIN.

Rylands's Hold Concert On

Their Behalf.

With the object of helping the local prisoners of war in Germany and at the same time taking the opportunity of presenting prizes to the employes who had been successful in the recent billiard handicaps, a sthoking concert was held in the Rylands's Recreation Pavilion on Satur- day evening. There was a large attendance. The prizes were distributed by Mr. W. Peter Rylands, who said that although he could not come amongst his men as of ten as he would like, owing to much of his time being taken up by his work, he always took a great interest in • them, and was always glad to get amongst them whenever he could.

Referring to the Daylight Savings Bill, Mr' Rylands thought it would be a good thing for the sportsmen if it were passed, because they would have an extra hour in which to play their summer games. Concluding with a reference te the war, Mr. Rylands thought that the workmen of Messrs. Rylands Bros. • had responded nobly to the call to arms, and he thought as great a percentage of men had enlisted from the works as from any other works in the country. (Applause.) Mr. Rylands then distributed the prizes to the successful handicap winners, and also a hand- some shield to the Steel Mill, the victors in the Inter-Department Handicap. The prize winners were ; Shield Competition.—Winners, Steel Mill: Messrs. R. Tilling (captain), S. Massey, E. Black-ledge, J. Gleays4 J. Tracey, W. Kerfoot, N. Cook, J. Unsworth, J. Massey and G. Shaw. Runners-up, Galvanising Department: Messrs. J. Sinclair (captain), W. Hardy, J. Gatenby, J. Ashton, J. Curry, H. Birehall, C. Crozier, W. Maybury. W. Daintith and E. Scott. Billiard Handicap: 1, E. Blackledge; 2, Private J. Crusham ; 3. W. Birch; 4. C. Crozier; 5, I. Newton; 6, W. Witton, 7, J. Woods; 8, A. Gibson; 9, G. Shaw; 10, IT. Langley; 11, L. Bate; 12, H. Lawton; 13. T. Warhurst ; 14, W. Ash- ton; 15, E. Skelland ; 16, H. Williams.

On the proposition of Mr. Baker, seconded by Mr. Critchley. a hearty vote of thanks wag accorded Mr. Rylands, while during the evening similar thanks were offered to Mr. T. Reddish. Mr. Phillips (who organised the concert), and to the artistes.

A Useful Contribution. Responding, Mr. W. H. Phillips said that as a result of the sale of tickets for the concert he would be able to hand over £5 to the Pris- oners of War Fund. He was always ready to do anything in his power for* the poor pris- oners, who were deserving of every attention. (Applause.) The concert was a very good one, and all the, items were well re:eived. Mr. F. Wolfenden and Mr. Wilde gave a pianoforte and concertina selection, while, later, Mr. Wilde gave another selection on the concertina. Mr. Gough sang "For the 'sake of our boys" and "Down in the mine," whilst Mr. H. Edwards delighted with his songs, comic and otherwise, "Puss in the corner" and "Telling the old, old story." Mr• J. Wrench had everybody in uproars with hie comic songs., "The night I fought Jack John- son," while Mr. Humphries gave "I fear no. foe" and "Blow, blow, thou winter wind." Mr. Hands contributed, in humorous style the songs "And we all went marching out" and "Taking. my father's tea." .Mr. F. Wolfeuden presided at the piano. Encores were frequent throughout the night.

WARRINGTON CORPORATION GAS DEPARTMENT. NOTICE TO COKE AND GAS CONSUMERS. COKE will be INCREASED to 11- per ewe!, subject to alteration without notice. The Government having requested us to reserve 'supplies for Munitions, it will be neces- sary to curtail supplies to other consumers. The Government request all consumers of light to reduce same 10 ner cent. W. S. IiADDOCK, General Manager. Gas Offices, Mersey-street, May 11th, 1916.

"An Army FiOhts On Its Stomach" If men are to fight well they must feed well. And not only must they have access to good food but they must have digestive systems that can easily assimilate it. An army of dyspeptics would be little more efficient than an army of starving men. The same principle holds good in other spheres of human endeavour. In business, for example, where competition grows kecner every year, no man can hope to make a permanent success who labours under the handicap of indigestion. The successful man is just the average man plus some extra energy, well directed, and energy is maintained by good food well digested. If you are conscious that your food is not so easily and thoroughly digested as it should be, you may, in the ordinary course, take it for granted that your stomach, liver, and bowels need a special corrective medicine, than which none is better than Beecham's Pills. This famous specific exerts a very marked stimulating effect upon the digestive organs and thereby tones up and invigorates the general health. Writing home, he says :—" You will be sur- prised to hear that I got wounded on the night of the sth April. It was a terrible night. I • got shot through the left elbow and the bullet, came through iny shoulder, breaking my arm. I lost a lot of blood, and have gone through some pain. I have been under one operation." Corporal Alcock enlisted in May, 1915, having been employed for seven years previously as a checker by Messrs. R. A. Naylor, Ltd. He is 30 years of age, and was educated at the People's College School. He has been at the Front nine months. PRIVATE T. EDMUNDS. Details Lacking Of ‘Vounds In Legs And Arm. Private Thomas Edmunds, of the K. 0.5.8. Regiment, has written to his aunt, Mrs. Lyndon, 25, Howley-lane, with whom he made his home, to say that he is wounded in the legs and arm. An official communication has also been re- ceived from the War Office, but full details are • lacking. Private Edmunds, who is 21 years of age, was educated at St. Alban's R.C.. School. He enlisted soon after the outbreak of war, having been previously employed by the Warrington Tanning Company.. RIFLEMAN J. FEARNLEV. , One Of Two Soldier-Brothers Returns To Duty. Rifleman John Fearnley, of 15, Antrobus- street, a member of the Rifle Brigade, was re- cently wounded, but has now sufficiently recovered to return to duty. Before enlisting, Private Fearnley was em- ployed by the London and North-Western Railway Company. He was educated at St. Alban's Roman Catholic School, and worshipped at St. Benedict's Church. His brother George enlisted along with him in the same regiment, and they had been together until John was wounded. DR. SUTHERLAND ILL. • Dr. Sutherland, the partner of Dr. Bennett, is seriously ill in a nursing 'home in London. He has been invalided home from the Front, where he has been since last August. Latest reports say that he is progressing favourably. • • .

considered that the mother was more to blame than her daugher, and she deserved a censure. It was stated ,that the woman's husband was a Sergeant-Major in the Army, and she was in receipt of £l. 19s. per week separation allow- ance, and there was no reason for her to go on in the way she had been doing. The girl was fined 10s., the Court ordering the mother to pay it, and warned her as to her future conduct. MUNITION WORKER IN THE CELLS. On Harry Byron, ironworker, of VO, Winwick- road, being again remanded for a week on a charge of wounding his mother-in-law, Mary Thompgon, of 373, Winwick-road, by kicking and striking her, Mr. T. S. Steel, for the prisoner, suggested that he might be granted bail. He was engaged on munition work and might be better employed on that than in staying in the cells. The Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls): It is a savage and brutal assault. The woman has two ribs broken, besides having internal injuries. Bail refused.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT .MEDAL COMPETITION. A meeting of the committee of the above com- petition was held on Tuesday night at the Mersey Hotel, Mr. G. Bomber presiding over a fair attendance of delegates. The Secretary reported that the final tie on. Saturday between Widnes Athletic and Win- wick- Rovers resulted in a drawn game of one goal each after two hours and twenty minutes' play. The balance sheet was also read and accepted , as satisfactory. It was decided to replay the final tie to-day on the Atherton Quay ground; kick off at 3.30. Officials: Referee, Mr. G. Bamber; linesmen, Mr. Hinds and Mr. Hatton. CHARITABLE FOOTBALLERS. Intermediate League Gets Nearly £37 • For War Funds. ?t' The committee of the Warrington Football ' Club have sent the Mayor a cheque for 5:18 6s. 3d., to be equally divided between the follow- ing funds :—Tobacco Fund for the 2./4th South ••-'; 1 Lanes. Regiment at the Front, the Prisoners of 1 War Fund, and the Voluntary Organisation Selteme. The money was obtained at the matches played by the :Intermediate League teams d Warring- - ton an Widnes on Good Friday and Easter , Monday respeetively. The "gate" money at War- I rington amounted to £2B is. 6d., and at Widnes £l5 18s. ed.. and after £7 7s. 6d. expenses, War- rington and Widnes had £lB 6s. 3d. each to give -to charity. This result is a very happy one for us to record. , BILLIARDS. STOCKTON HEATH ADULT SCHOOL v. CHESHIRE LINES RAILWAY. Played at Stockton Heath on Tuesday. Scores: Stockton Heath team: W. DISTRICT TRIBUNAL. Married Men Explain Their Hard Lot. A sitting of the Warrington District Tribunal was held at the Council Offices on Wednesday afternoon. Mr. G. Mather presided, and a number of appeals by married men came under review. SOAP WORKER AND AGRICULTURIST. Mr. H. Woods, representing a Rixton married man employed at a Manchester soap works, applied for exemption on the ground that the work he was doing was very heavy and could not be done by women, old men, or boys. He was also engaged in farm work. Mr. Woods put forward appellant's agricultural work as the chief reason for his exemption, rather than his other business. The appeal was refused. YOUNGEST GOT TO GO. A Rixton woman aged 70, farming 37 acres of land, appealed for her two grandsons, aged 18, single, and 29, married, who, she said, were in- dispensable for carrying on the farm. Mr. Steel represented the appellant. The Tribunal decided that the youngest man should join the forces, and granted the eldest conditional exemption. NEED FOR FARRIERS. A Burtonwood blacksmith and farrier was given conditional exemption. NOT TO APPEAL AGAIN. "Your son will be given exemption for one month, but we will not listen to another appeal," said the Chairman in disposing of an appeal by a Horton Green farmer for his son, aged 23 years. In reply to Mr. Furness, appellant said he had three sons, but none had joined the Forces. SEVEN CHILDREN TO MAINTAIN. A married man, aged 33, with a wife in a delicate state of health and seven children, all under the age of 12, urged that it was in the national interests that he should stay at home to look after the welfare of his wife and chil- dren. The Tribunal agreed, and gave him con- ditional exemption. AWKWARD IF NO BOOT REPAIRER. A Penketh boot repairer, pleading for exemp- tion, said if he had to join his business wouid have to be closed. He was the only repairer in the village. A member said it would be very awkward if they could not get their boots repaired.—Con- ditional exemption was granted. A NATIONAL NECESSITY. A Fearnhead master-butcher and slaughter- man, was given conditional exemption. He said he was a married man with two children. He had built up a business with an extensive round and he kept two horses. He also assiste.l other butchers in slaughtering. Appellant said it was in the national interests that he should stay at home to supply food. He had also domestic liabilities to meet. WORK WOMEN CANNOT DO. A boiler cleaner, aged 25, employed at th 3 Burtonwood Brewery, was granted three months' exemption. The employer said a woman could not do the work, and he had been unable to get anyone to take the man's place
